Minimal Session Types for the π \pi -calculus
Alen Arslanagić, Jorge A. Pérez, Anda-Amelia Palamariuc

Abstract
Session types are a discipline for the static verification of message-passing programs. A session type specifies a channel’s protocol as sequences of exchanges. It is most relevant to investigate session-based concurrency by identifying the essential notions that enable program specification and verification. Following that perspective, prior work identified minimal session types (MSTs), a sub-class of session types without the sequentiality construct, which specifies the structure of communication actions. This formulation of session types led to establish a minimality result : every process typable with standard session types can be compiled down to a process typable using MSTs, which mimics sequentiality in types via additional process synchronizations. Such a minimality result is significant because it justifies session types in terms of themselves, without resorting to external notions; it was proven for a higher-order session π \pi -calculus, in which values are abstractions (functions from names to processes). In this paper, we study MSTs and their associated minimality result but now for the session π \pi -calculus, the (first-order) language in which values are names and for which session types have been more widely studied. We first show that this new minimality result can be obtained by composing known results. Then, we develop optimizations of this new minimality result and prove that the associated transformation into processes with MSTs satisfies dynamic correctness.

stix@largesymbols"0F

1
Introduction

Session types are a type-based approach to statically ensure that message-passing programs correctly implement some predefined protocols
[
9
,
10
]
.
A session type stipulates the sequence and payload of the messages exchanged along a channel.
Sequentiality, denoted by the action prefix ‘
\,;\,
’, is arguably the most distinctive construct of session types, as it allows to specify structured communications.
For instance, in the session type
S=?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
, this construct conveniently specifies a channel protocol that
first
receives (‘
?
’) two integers,
then
sends (‘
!
’) a Boolean, and
finally
ends.

Because sequentiality is so useful for protocol specification and verification, a natural question is whether it could be recovered by other, simpler means.
To investigate this question, Arslanagić et al.
[
3
]
defined
minimal session types
(MSTs), an alternative formulation of session types.
The difference between standard and minimal session types concerns sequentiality in types: MSTs is the sub-class of session types without sequentiality.
To see the difference, consider session types for input and output, denoted ‘
!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
’ and ‘
?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
’, respectively: in standard session types, the type
S
denotes an arbitrary (session) protocol; in MSTs, the type
S
can only be ‘
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
’, the type of the terminated protocol.
Arslanagić et al. established a
minimality result
: every well-typed session process can be
decomposed
into a process typable with MSTs. Their approach to decomposition is inspired by Parrow’s work on
trios processes
for the untyped
\pi
-calculus
[
18
]
. The minimality result justifies session types in terms of themselves, without resorting to external notions, and shows that sequentiality in types is useful but not indispensable, because it can be precisely mimicked by the process decomposition.

The minimality result based on MSTs in
[
3
]
was proven for
\mathsf{HO}
, a
higher-order
process calculus in which values are abstractions (functions from names to processes).
\mathsf{HO}
does not include name passing nor process recursion, but it can encode them precisely
[
13
,
15
]
. An important question left open in
[
3
]
is whether the minimality result holds for a
first-order
session
\pi
-calculus, i.e., a language in which values are names. This is a relevant question, as session types have been more widely studied in the first-order setting, from foundational and practical angles. Unlike
\mathsf{HO}
, the session
\pi
-calculus we consider, dubbed
\pi
in the following, natively supports recursion and recursive types.

In this paper, we report
two positive answers
to this question.
Our
first answer
is simple, perhaps even deceptively so. In order to establish the minimality result for
\pi
, we compose the decomposition in
[
3
]
with the mutual encodings between
\mathsf{HO}
and
\pi
given in
[
13
,
15
]
.
We call this the
decompose by composing
approach.

\pi
\mu\pi
\mathsf{HO}
\mu\mathsf{HO}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}

Figure 1:
First approach to a minimality result for
\pi
: Decompose by Composing.

More precisely, let
\mu\pi
and
\mu\mathsf{HO}
denote the process languages
\pi
and
\mathsf{HO}
with MSTs (rather than with standard session types).
Also, let
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
denote the decomposition function from
\mathsf{HO}
to
\mu\mathsf{HO}
defined in
[
3
]
.
Kouzapas et al.
[
13
,
15
]
gave typed encodings from
\pi
to
\mathsf{HO}
(denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
) and from
\mathsf{HO}
to
\pi
(denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
).
Therefore, given
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
, to define a decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}:\pi\to\mu\pi
, it suffices to compose these three functions following
Figure

1
.
This is sound for our purposes, because
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
preserve sequentiality in processes and their types.

The correctness of the decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
follows from that of its constituent functions.
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
is significant, as it provides an elegant, positive answer to the question of whether the minimality result in
[
3
]
holds for
\pi
and all its constructs, including recursion. Indeed, it proves that the kind of values exchanged do not influence sequentiality in session types: the minimality result of
[
3
]
is not specific to the abstraction-passing language
\mathsf{HO}
.

Unfortunately,
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
is not entirely satisfactory. A side effect of composing
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
is that the resulting decomposition of
\pi
into
\mu\pi
exhibits some
suboptimal features
, in particular redundant synchronizations.
These shortcomings are particularly noticeable in the treatment of processes with recursion and recursive types. The
second answer
to the open question from
[
3
]
is an optimized variant of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
, dubbed
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, which avoids redundant synchronizations and treats recursive processes and variables directly, exploiting the fact that
\pi
supports recursion natively.

Contributions.

The main contributions of this paper are:

1.

Two new minimality results for
\pi
. The first leverages the function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
, obtained by following the “decompose by composing” approach in
Figure

1
(
Theorem

3.1
); the second exploits
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, an optimized variant of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
without redundant communications (
Theorem

4.1
). Also,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
provides direct support for recursion and recursive types.
We provide metrics for the improvements in moving from
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
(
Lemma

4.1
).

2.

The minimality results are, in essence, a guarantee of
static correctness
for our decompositions. In the case of the optimized decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, we complement this static guarantee with a
dynamic correctness
guarantee: following
[
2
]
, we prove that well-typed processes
P
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
are behaviorally equivalent (
Theorem

4.2
).

3.

Examples that illustrate the workings of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.

Organization.

The rest of this paper is organized as follows.
Next,
Section

2
collects definitions and results from prior works, useful to our developments.

Section

3
develops the “decompose by composing” approach, and presents the first minimality result for
\pi
.

Section

4
reports the optimized decomposition function, our second minimality result, and its dynamic correctness guarantee.

Section

5
discusses by example the extension of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
with constructs for labeled choices (selection and branching).

Section

6
discusses related works, and in particular compares our approach and results against the work by Dardha et al.
[
5
,
6
,
4
]
.
Finally,
Section

7
concludes.

Omitted technical material appears in the appendices.
Throughout the paper, we use colors: they are meant to facilitate reading, but are not indispensable for following the text. In particular, we use

green
color for notions from prior works, and

red
and
blue
colors to distinguish elements of our first and second decompositions, respectively.

Origin of the Results.

This paper is an extended and revised version of a conference paper that appeared at PPDP’21
[
1
]
and was presented as a short paper at EXPRESS/SOS’21. With respect to
[
1
]
, the current presentation includes complete technical details and extended examples; in particular,
Section

5
is new and
Section

6
has been substantially extended.

Preliminaries

In this section, we recall the syntax, semantics, and session type system of
\mathsf{HO}\pi

[
14
,
15
]
.
1
1

1

We consider
\mathsf{HO}\pi
without labeled choices (selection and branching); these constructs will be discussed in
Section

5
.

As mentioned above, we are concerned with
\pi
, which is the first-order sub-language of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
.
We also recall the mutual encodings between
\pi
and

\mathsf{HO}
, the other relevant sub-language of
\mathsf{HO}\pi

[
13
,
15
]
.
Finally, we briefly discuss MSTs for
\mathsf{HO}
and overview the minimality result in
[
3
,
2
]
.

2.1
\mathsf{HO}\pi
(and its Sub-languages
\mathsf{HO}
and
\pi
)

\displaystyle a,b\;\;{|}\;\;s,\overline{s}

\displaystyle u,w\;::=\;

\displaystyle n\;\;{|}\;\;x,y,z

\displaystyle V,W\;::=\;

\displaystyle\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$u$}\;\;{|}\;\;\fcolorbox{black}{white}{\ensuremath{\abs{x}{P}}}\;\;|\;\;\fcolorbox{black}{white}{\ensuremath{x,y,z}}

\displaystyle P,Q\;::=\;

\displaystyle u!\langle V\rangle.{P}\;\;|\;\;u?(x).{P}\;\;|\;\;\fcolorbox{black}{white}{\ensuremath{\appl{V}{u}}}\;\;|\;\;P\;|\;Q\;\;|\;\;(\nu\,n)\,P\;\;|\;\;\mathbf{0}\;\;|\;\;\hbox{\pagecolor{lightgray}$X\;\;|\;\;\mu X.P$}

Syntax.

Figure

2
gives the syntax of processes
P,Q,\dots
, values
V,W,\ldots
, and conventions for names. Identifiers
a,b,c,\dots
denote
shared names
, while
s,\overline{s},\dots
are used for
session names
.
Names
(shared or sessions) are denoted by
m,n\dots
, and
x,y,z,\dots
range over variables. A notion of duality applies to names: the dual of
n
is denoted
\overline{n}
. Duality is defined only on session names; this way,
\overline{\overline{s}}=s
, but
\overline{a}=a
.

We write
\widetilde{x}
to denote a tuple
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n})
, and use
\epsilon
to denote the empty tuple. Given a tuple
\widetilde{x}
, we use
|\widetilde{x}|
to denote its length.
With a slight abuse of notation, we sometimes write
\widetilde{x}
to refer to its associated finite set of elements (and vice versa).

An abstraction
\lambda x.\,P
binds
x
to its body
P
. In processes, sequentiality is specified via prefixes. The
output
prefix,
u!\big\langle V\big\rangle.P
, sends value
V
on name
u
, then continues as
P
. Its dual is the
input
prefix,
u?(x).P
, in which variable
x
is bound. In the following we shall consider
polyadic
communication (i.e., communication of tuples of values), and so we have output and input prefixes of the form
u!\big\langle\widetilde{V}\big\rangle.P
and
u?(\widetilde{x}).P
, respectively.
Parallel composition,
P\;|\;Q
, specifies the combined behavior of two processes running simultaneously. Restriction
(\nu\,n)\,P
binds the endpoints
n,\overline{n}
in process
P
.
Process
\mathbf{0}
denotes inaction.
Recursive variables and recursive processes are denoted
X
and
\mu X.P
, respectively.
Replication is denoted by the shorthand notation
\ast\,{P}
, which stands for
\mu X.(P\;|\;X)
.

The sets of free variables, sessions, and names of a process are denoted
\mathsf{fv}(P)
,
\mathsf{fs}(P)
, and
\mathsf{fn}(P)
. A process
P
is
closed
if
\mathsf{fv}(P)=\emptyset
.
We write
u!\big\langle\big\rangle.P
and
u?().P
when the communication objects are not relevant. Also, we omit trailing occurrences of
\mathbf{0}
.

As shown in
Figure

2
, the sub-languages
\pi
and
\mathsf{HO}
of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
differ as follows:
application
Vu
is only present in
\mathsf{HO}
; constructs for recursion
\mu X.P
are present in
\pi
but not in
\mathsf{HO}
.

\displaystyle n!\langle V\rangle.P\;|\;\overline{n}?(x).Q

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P\;|\;Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Pass}]}}

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}\Rightarrow(\nu\,n)\,P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow(\nu\,n)\,P^{\prime}

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Res}]}}

\displaystyle P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}\Rightarrow P\;|\;Q

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P^{\prime}\;|\;Q

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Par}]}}

\displaystyle P\equiv Q\longrightarrow Q^{\prime}\equiv P^{\prime}\Rightarrow P

\displaystyle\longrightarrow P^{\prime}

\displaystyle{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Cong}]}}

\displaystyle P_{1}\;|\;P_{2}\equiv P_{2}\;|\;P_{1}\quad P_{1}\;|\;(P_{2}\;|\;P_{3})\equiv(P_{1}\;|\;P_{2})\;|\;P_{3}

\displaystyle P\;|\;\mathbf{0}\equiv P\quad P\;|\;(\nu\,n)\,Q\equiv(\nu\,n)\,(P\;|\;Q)\penalty\ \penalty\ (n\notin\mathsf{fn}(P))

Reduction Semantics of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
.

Reduction Semantics.

The operational semantics of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
, enclosed in
Figure

3
, is expressed through a
reduction relation
, denoted
\longrightarrow
.
Reduction is closed under
structural congruence
,
\equiv
, which identifies equivalent processes from a structural perspective.
We write
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}
to denote the capture-avoiding substitution of variable
x
with value
V
in
P
.
As customary, the capture-avoiding, simultaneous substitution in the polyadic setting is denoted
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{V}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}$}\}
, with the assumption that
|\widetilde{V}|=|\widetilde{x}|
.
We write
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime},\ldots
to range over substitutions, and
write ‘
\{\}
’ to denote the empty substitution.
In
Figure

3
, Rule
{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small App}]}}
denotes application, which only concerns names.
Rule
{\scriptsize{[\textrm{\small Pass}]}}
defines a shared or session interaction on channel
n
’s endpoints. The remaining rules are standard.

We shall often write
P\longrightarrow^{k}P^{\prime}
to denote a sequence of
k>0
reductions between
P
and
P^{\prime}
.

Labeled Transition System (LTS).

We define the interaction of processes with their environment using action labels
\ell
:

\ell\;::=\;\tau\;\;{|}\;\;(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle\;\;{|}\;\;n?\langle V\rangle

Label
\tau
defines internal actions.
Action

(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle

denotes the sending of value
V

over channel
n
with a possible empty set of restricted names

\widetilde{m}

(we may write
n!\langle V\rangle
when
\widetilde{m}
is empty).
Dually, the action for value reception is

n?\langle V\rangle
.
We write
\mathsf{fn}(\ell)
and
\mathsf{bn}(\ell)
to denote the
sets of free/bound names in
\ell
, respectively.
Given
\ell\neq\tau
, we
say
\ell
is a
visible action
; we
write
\mathtt{subj}(\ell)

to denote its
subject
.
This way, we have:

\mathtt{subj}((\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle)=\mathtt{subj}(n?\langle V\rangle)=n
.

Dual actions

occur on subjects that are dual between them and carry the same
object; thus, output is dual to input. We define duality on actions
as the least symmetric relation
\asymp
on action labels that satisfies:

(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle\asymp\overline{n}?\langle V\rangle

\frac{}{(\lambda x.\,P)\,{V}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{App}\rangle$}}\frac{}{n!\langle V\rangle.P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n!\langle V\rangle}}P}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{Snd}\rangle$}}\frac{}{n?(x).P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?\langle V\rangle}}P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$V$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{Rv}\rangle$}}\frac{P\equiv_{\alpha}QQ\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}}{P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{Alpha}\rangle$}}\frac{P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}n\notin\mathsf{fn}(\ell)}{(\nu\,n)\,P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}(\nu\,n)\,P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{Res}\rangle$}}\frac{P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle}}P^{\prime}m_{1}\in\mathsf{fn}(V)}{(\nu\,m_{1})\,P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,m_{1},\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle}}P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{New}\rangle$}}\frac{P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}\mathsf{bn}(\ell)\cap\mathsf{fn}(Q)=\emptyset}{P\;|\;Q\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}\;|\;Q}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{Par${}_{L}$}\rangle$}}\frac{P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell_{1}}}P^{\prime}Q\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell_{2}}}Q^{\prime}\ell_{1}\asymp\ell_{2}}{P\;|\;Q\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}(\nu\,\mathsf{bn}(\ell_{1})\cup\mathsf{bn}(\ell_{2}))(P^{\prime}\;|\;Q^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{Tau}\rangle$}}\frac{P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mu X.P$}\!/\mbox{\small$X$}\}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}}{\mu X.P\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize$\langle\textsc{Rec}\rangle$}}

Figure 4:
The Untyped LTS for
\mathsf{HO}\pi
processes. We omit Rule
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Par${}_{R}$}\rangle}
.

The (early) labeled transition system
(LTS) for
untyped
processes
is given in

Figure

4
.
We write
P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P_{2}
with the usual meaning.
The rules are standard
[
17
,
16
]
; we comment on some of them.
A process with an output prefix can
interact with the environment with an output action that carries a value

V
(Rule
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Snd}\rangle}
). Dually, in Rule
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Rv}\rangle}
a
receiver process can observe an input of an arbitrary value
V
.
Rule
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Res}\rangle}

enables an observable action from a process with an outermost restriction, provided that
the restricted name does not occur free in the action.
If a restricted name occurs free in
the carried value of an output action,
the process performs scope opening (Rule
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{New}\rangle}
).
Rule
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Rec}\rangle}
handles recursion unfolding.
Rule
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Tau}\rangle}

states that two parallel processes which perform
dual actions can synchronise by an internal transition.
Rules
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Par${}_{L}$}\rangle}
/
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Par${}_{R}$}\rangle}

and
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Alpha}\rangle}

define standard treatments for actions under parallel composition and
\alpha
-renaming.

In defining (typed) behavioral equivalences for processes, later on we shall consider a
typed
LTS, i.e., an enhancement of the untyped LTS in

Figure

4
with session types.

2.2
Session Types for
\mathsf{HO}\pi

\displaystyle U\;::=\;

\displaystyle C\;\;{|}\;\;L

\displaystyle C\;::=\;

\displaystyle S\;\;{|}\;\;\langle S\rangle\;\;{|}\;\;\langle L\rangle

\displaystyle L\;::=\;

\displaystyle U\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\;\;{|}\;\;U\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle S\;::=\;

\displaystyle!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\;\;{|}\;\;?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\;\;{|}\;\;\mu\mathsf{t}.S\;\;{|}\;\;\mathsf{t}\;\;{|}\;\;\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

.

\displaystyle U\;::=\;

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\;\;{|}\;\;\widetilde{C}\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle C\;::=\;

\displaystyle M\;\;{|}\;\;{\langle U\rangle}

\displaystyle\gamma\;::=\;

\displaystyle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\;\;{|}\;\;\mathsf{t}

\displaystyle M\;::=\;

\displaystyle\gamma\;\;{|}\;\;!\langle\widetilde{U}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\gamma\;\;{|}\;\;?(\widetilde{U}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\gamma\;\;{|}\;\;\mu\mathsf{t}.M

Figure 5:
STs for
\mathsf{HO}\pi
(top) and MSTs for
\mathsf{HO}
(bottom).

Figure

5
(top) gives the syntax of types.
Value types
U
include
the first-order types
C
and the higher-order
types
L
.
In our examples we use other value (base) types, such as
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}
and
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}
.

Session types are denoted with
S
and
shared types with
\langle S\rangle
and
\langle L\rangle
.
We write
\diamond
to denote the
process type
.
The functional types
U\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
and
U\!\multimap\!\diamond
denote

shared
and
linear
higher-order
types, respectively.
The
output type

!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
is assigned to a name that
first sends a value of
type
U
and then follows the type described by
S
.
Dually,
?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
denotes an
input type
.
Type
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
is the termination type.
We assume the
recursive type

\mu\mathsf{t}.S
is guarded,
i.e., the type variable
\mathsf{t}
only appears under prefixes.
This way, types such as,
e.g., the type
\mu\mathsf{t}.\mathsf{t}
are not allowed.
The sets of free/bound variables of a type
S
are defined as usual;
the sole binder is
\mu\mathsf{t}.S
.
Closed session types do not have free type variables.

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\{u:S\}\vdash u\triangleright S}\quad\textsc{(Sess)}\qquad\frac{}{\Gamma,u:U;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright U}\quad\textsc{(Sh)}\\
\\
\frac{}{\Gamma;\{x:C\!\multimap\!\diamond\};\emptyset\vdash x\triangleright C\!\multimap\!\diamond}\quad\textsc{(LVar)}\qquad\frac{}{\Gamma,X:\Delta;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash X\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(RVar)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash x\triangleright C}{\Gamma\backslash x;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\backslash\Delta_{2}\vdash\lambda{x}.\,P\triangleright{C}\!\multimap\!\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Abs)}\quad\frac{{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash V\triangleright C\!\leadsto\!\diamond\penalty\ \penalty\ \leadsto\,\in\{\multimap,\rightarrow\}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash u\triangleright C}}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash V\,{u}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(App)}\par\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash V\triangleright C\!\multimap\!\diamond}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash V\triangleright C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Prom)}\quad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda,x:C\!\multimap\!\diamond;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma,x:C\!\rightarrow\!\diamond;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(EProm)}\quad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447ptu\not\in\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta)}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(End)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma,X:\Delta;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash\mu X.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Rec)}\qquad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda_{i};\Delta_{i}\vdash P_{i}\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pti=1,2}{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash P_{1}\;|\;P_{2}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Par)}\qquad\frac{}{\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash\mathbf{0}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Nil)}\\
\\
\frac{u:S\in\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash V\triangleright U}{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2};((\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2})\setminus u:S),u:!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u!\langle V\rangle.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Send)}\\
\\
\frac{{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright\langle{U}\rangle\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash V\triangleright{U}\hskip 8.19447pt(U=S)\lor(U=L)}}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash u!\langle V\rangle.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Req)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1},u:S\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash{x}\triangleright{U}}{\Gamma\backslash x;\Lambda_{1}\backslash\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1}\backslash\Delta_{2},u:?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u?({x}).P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Rcv)}\penalty\ \penalty\ \frac{\begin{array}[]{c}{\Gamma;\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright\langle{U}\rangle}\quad{\Gamma;\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash x\triangleright{U}\quad\quad(U=S)\lor(U=L)}\end{array}}{\Gamma\backslash x;\Lambda_{1}\backslash\Lambda_{2};\Delta_{1}\backslash\Delta_{2}\vdash u?(x).P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Acc)}\\
\\
\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,s:S_{1},\overline{s}:S_{2}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447ptS_{1}\ \mathsf{dual}\ S_{2}}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,s)\,P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(ResS)}\qquad\frac{\Gamma,a:\langle S\rangle;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,a)\,P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Res)}\end{array}

Figure 6:
Typing Rules for
\mathsf{HO}\pi
.

Session types for
\pi
exclude
L
from value types
U
and
\langle L\rangle
from
C
; session types for
\mathsf{HO}
exclude
C
from value types
U
.

We write
S_{1}\ \mathsf{dual}\ S_{2}
if

S_{1}
is the
dual
of
S_{2}
.
Intuitively,
duality
converts
!
into
?
(and vice-versa).
This intuitive definition is enough for our purposes; the formal definition is co-inductive, see
[
15
,
14
]
.
Typing
environments
are defined below:

\displaystyle\Gamma

\displaystyle\;::=\;\emptyset\;\;{|}\;\;\Gamma,x:U\!\rightarrow\!\diamond\;\;{|}\;\;\Gamma,u:\langle S\rangle\;\;{|}\;\;\Gamma,u:\langle L\rangle\;\;{|}\;\;\Gamma,X:\Delta

\displaystyle\Lambda

\displaystyle\;::=\;\emptyset\;\;{|}\;\;\Lambda,x\!:\!U\!\multimap\!\diamond

\displaystyle\Delta

\displaystyle\;::=\;\emptyset\;\;{|}\;\;\Delta,u\!:\!S

We shall refer to
\Delta
as the
session environment
.

\Gamma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Delta
satisfy different structural principles.

\Gamma
maps variables and shared names to value types, and recursive
variables to session environments;
it admits weakening, contraction, and exchange principles.

\Lambda
maps variables to
linear higher-order
types, and so it is relevant only for processes featuring passing of abstractions.

\Delta
maps
session names to session types.
Both
\Lambda
and
\Delta
are
only subject to exchange.
The domains of
\Gamma
,

\Lambda
and
\Delta

(denoted
\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma)
,
\mathsf{dom}(\Lambda)
, and
\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)
, respectively)
are assumed pairwise distinct.

Given
\Gamma
, we write
\Gamma\backslash x
to denote the environment obtained from

\Gamma
by removing the assignment
x:U\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
, for some
U
.
This notation applies similarly to
\Delta
and
\Lambda
;
we write
\Delta\backslash\Delta^{\prime}
(and
\Lambda\backslash\Lambda^{\prime}
) with the expected meaning.
Notation

\Delta_{1}\cdot\Delta_{2}
means
the disjoint union of
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
.
We define
typing judgements
for values
V

and processes
P
:

\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash V\triangleright U\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

The judgement on the left
says that under environments
\Gamma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Delta
value
V

has type
U
; the judgement on the right says that under
environments
\Gamma
,
\Lambda
, and
\Delta
process
P
has the process type
\diamond
.
The typing rules are presented in
Figure

6
.

Type soundness for
\mathsf{HO}\pi
relies on two auxiliary notions:

Definition 2.1

(Session Environments: Balanced/Reduction)
.

Let
\Delta
be a session environment.

\bullet

\Delta
is
balanced
, written
\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta)
, if whenever

s:S_{1},\overline{s}:S_{2}\in\Delta
then
S_{1}\ \mathsf{dual}\ S_{2}
.

\bullet

We define reduction
\longrightarrow
on session environments as:

\displaystyle\Delta,s:!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S_{1},\overline{s}:?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S_{2}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\Delta,s:S_{1},\overline{s}:S_{2}

Theorem 2.1

(Type Soundness
[
14
]
)
.

Suppose
\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

with

\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta)
.
Then
P\longrightarrow P^{\prime}
implies
\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta^{\prime}\vdash P^{\prime}\triangleright\diamond

and
\Delta=\Delta^{\prime}
or
\Delta\longrightarrow\Delta^{\prime}

with
\mathsf{balanced}(\Delta^{\prime})
.

2.3
Typed Encodings between
\pi
and
\mathsf{HO}

The encodings
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}:\pi\to\mathsf{HO}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}:\mathsf{HO}\to\pi
are
typed
: each consists of a translation on processes and a translation on types.
This way,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{1}
translates types for first-order processes into types for higher-order processes, while
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2}
operates in the opposite direction—see Figures
8

and
9
, respectively.
Remarkably, these translations on processes and types do not alter their sequentiality.

From
\pi
to
\mathsf{HO}
.

To mimic the sending of name
w
, the encoding
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
encloses
w

within the body of an input-guarded abstraction. The corresponding input process
receives this higher-order value, applies it on a restricted session, and sends
the encoded continuation through the session’s dual.

Several auxiliary notions are used to encode recursive processes; we describe
them intuitively (see
[
15
]
for full details).
The key idea is to encode recursive processes in
\pi
using a “duplicator”
process in
\mathsf{HO}
, circumventing linearity by replacing free names with variables.
The parameter
g
is a map from process variables to sequences of name
variables.
To handle linearity, auxiliary mappings are defined:

|\!|{\cdot}|\!|
maps sequences of session names into sequences
of variables, and
\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor\cdot\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\emptyset}
maps processes with free names to
processes without free names (but with free variables instead):

Definition 2.2

(Auxiliary Mappings)
.

We define mappings
|\!|\cdot|\!|
and
\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor\cdot\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}
as follows:

•

|\!|\cdot|\!|:\mathcal{N}^{\omega}\longrightarrow\mathcal{V}^{\omega}

is a map of sequences of lexicographically ordered names to sequences of variables, defined
inductively
as:

\displaystyle|\!|\epsilon|\!|

\displaystyle=\epsilon

\displaystyle|\!|n,\tilde{m}|\!|

\displaystyle=x_{n},|\!|\tilde{m}|\!|\quad\text{($x$ fresh)}

•

Given a set of session names and variables
\Psi
,
the map

\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor\cdot\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}:\mathsf{HO}\to\mathsf{HO}

is as in
Figure

7
.

The encoding
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{1}
depends on the auxiliary function

{\big\lfloor\cdot\big\rfloor^{1}}
, defined on value types. Following the encoding on processes,
this mapping on values takes a first-order value type and encodes it into a
linear higher-order value type, which encloses an input type that expects to
receive another higher-order type. Notice how the innermost higher-order value
type is either shared or linear, following the nature of the given type.

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor w!\langle\lambda x.\,Q\rangle.P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u!\langle\lambda x.\,\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor Q\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi,x}\rangle.\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor w\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u\triangleright\{l_{i}:\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor P_{i}\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor w?(x).P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u?(x).\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor w\triangleleft l.P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u\triangleleft l.\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor(\nu\,n)\,P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}(\nu\,n)\,\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi,n}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor(\lambda x.Q)\,{w}\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}(\lambda x.\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor Q\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi,x})\,{u}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor P\;|\;Q\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor P\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}\;|\;\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor Q\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor x\,{w}\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}x\,{u}

\displaystyle\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor\mathbf{0}\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\Psi}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}\mathbf{0}

In all cases:
u=\begin{cases}x_{n}&\text{if $w$ is a name $n$ and $n\not\in\Psi$ ($x$ fresh)}\\
w&\text{otherwise: $w$ is a variable or a name $n$ and $n\in\Psi$}\end{cases}

Typed encoding of
\pi
into
\mathsf{HO}
, selection from
[
15
]
.
Above,

\mathsf{fn}(P)
is a lexicographically ordered sequence of free names in
P
.
Maps
|\!|\cdot|\!|
and

and

.

From
\mathsf{HO}
to
\pi
.

The encoding
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
simulates higher-order communication using first-order constructs, following Sangiorgi
[
19
]
. The idea is to use
trigger names
, which point towards copies of input-guarded server processes that should be activated.
The encoding of abstraction sending distinguishes two cases: if the abstraction body does not contain any free session names (which are linear), then the server can be replicated.
Otherwise, if the value contains session names then its corresponding server name must be used exactly once.
The encoding of abstraction receiving proceeds inductively, noticing that the variable is now a placeholder for a first-order name.
The encoding of application is also in two cases; both of them depend on the creation of a fresh session, which is used to pass around the applied name.

Terms:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}u!\langle\lambda x.\,Q\rangle.P{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}\begin{cases}(\nu\,a)(u!\langle a\rangle.({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}\;|\;\ast\,{a?(y).y?(x).{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}}))&\text{if $\mathsf{fs}(Q)=\emptyset$}\\
&\text{\penalty\ with $*P=\mu X.(P\;|\;X)$}\\
(\nu\,a)(u!\langle a\rangle.({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}\;|\;a?(y).y?(x).{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}))&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}u?(x).P{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u?(x).{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}x\,{u}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}(\nu\,s)(x!\langle s\rangle.\overline{s}!\langle u\rangle.\mathbf{0})

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}(\lambda x.\,P)\,{u}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}(\nu\,s)(s?(x).{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle u\rangle.\mathbf{0})

Types:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}!\langle S\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2}

Typed encoding of
\mathsf{HO}
into

[

]
.

2.4
Minimal Session Types and the Minimality Result for
\mathsf{HO}

As mentioned above, MSTs are session types without sequencing: in session types such as

!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
and
?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
, the type
S
can only correspond to
\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
.
The syntax of MSTs for
\mathsf{HO}
is in
Figure

5
(bottom).
Recall that we write
\mu\mathsf{HO}
to denote
\mathsf{HO}
with MSTs.
The decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
in
[
3
]
relies crucially on polyadic communication.
Hence, following
[
13
,
15
]
, value types are of the form
\widetilde{C}\!\rightarrow\!\diamond
and
\widetilde{C}\!\multimap\!\diamond
. Similarly, minimal session types for output and input are of the form
!\langle\widetilde{U}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
and
?(\widetilde{U}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
: they communicate tuples of values but lack a continuation.

Trios Processes.

Following Parrow
[
18
]
, we define the decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
on
\mathsf{HO}
processes in terms of a
breakdown function

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}
, which translates a process into a composition of
trios processes
(or simply
trios
).
A trio is a process with exactly three nested prefixes.
If
P
is a sequential process with
k
nested actions, then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
will contain
k
trios running in parallel: each trio in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
will enact exactly one prefix from
P
; the breakdown function must be carefully designed to ensure that trios trigger each other in such a way that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
preserves the prefix sequentiality in
P
.
While trios decompositions elegantly induce processes typable with MSTs, they are not a goal in themselves; rather, they offer one possible path to better understand sequentiality in session types.

We use some useful terminology from
[
18
]
.
The
context
of a trio is a tuple of variables
\widetilde{x}
, possibly empty, which makes variable bindings explicit.
We use a reserved set of
propagator names
(or simply
propagators
), denoted by
c_{k},c_{k+1},\ldots
, to carry contexts and trigger the
next trio.
Propagators with recursive types are denoted by
c_{k}^{r},c_{k+1}^{r},\ldots
.
We say that a
leading trio
is the one that receives a context, performs an action, and triggers the next one;
a
control trio
only activates other trios.

The Breakdown Function: Preliminaries

The breakdown function works on both processes and values.
The breakdown of process
P
is denoted by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}
, where
k
is the
index for the propagator
c_{k}
, and
\widetilde{x}
is the context to be received by the previous trio.
Similarly,
the breakdown of a value
V
is denoted by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{V}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}
.

Table

1
gives
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{V}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}
as defined in
[
3
]
.
As we explain below, the decompositions exploit type information. Hence, in the table we include side conditions derived from typing; notice that for session types we have either
C=S
or
C=\langle S\rangle
.

Table

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}

\eta_{1},\eta_{2}

\eta_{1}=(c)\,\mathsf{::}\,\eta_{2}\mathbf{:}\,\eta_{3}

to express that the equality
\eta_{1}=\eta_{2}
holds if the Boolean condition
c
is true, and that the equality
\eta_{1}=\eta_{3}
holds otherwise.

We say that a process has been
initialized

indexed names
(i.e., all of its names have some index).

Definition 2.3

(Predicates on Types (and Names))
.

Given a session type
C
, we write
\mathsf{tr}(C)
to indicate that
C
is a
tail-recursive session type, and
\mathsf{lin}(C)
otherwise.
Given
u:C
, we write
\mathsf{lin}(u)
if
C=S
and
\neg\mathsf{tr}(S)

and
\mathsf{tr}(u)
otherwise.

Definition 2.4

.

Let
n_{i}

\mathsf{next}(n_{i})=(\mathsf{lin}(n_{i}))\,\mathsf{::}\,\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$n_{i+1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$n_{i}$}\}\mathbf{:}\,\{\}
.

Definition

2.6
)

In order to determine the required number of propagators (
c_{k},c_{k+1},\ldots
) required in the breakdown of processes and values, we define the
degree
of a process:

Definition 2.5

(Degree of a Process)
.

Let
P
be an
\mathsf{HO}
process.
The
degree
of
P
, denoted
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}}
, is defined as
follows:

{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}}=\begin{cases}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}}+1&\text{if $P=u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.Q$ or $P=u_{i}?(y).Q$}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P^{\prime}}\rbag}}&\text{if $P=(\nu\,s:S)\,P^{\prime}$}\\
{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}}+{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}R}\rbag}}+1&\text{if $P=Q\;|\;R$}\\
1&\text{if $P=V\,{u_{i}}$ or $P=\mathbf{0}$}\end{cases}

The Breakdown Function: Intutions

The breakdown function relies on type information: names are decomposed based on their session types; also, the shape of decomposed process depends on whether the associated session type is tail-recursive or not.
Moreover, we shall rely on decompositions/functions on types, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
, which will be defined later on, in

Definition

2.6
.
Next, we describe the most interesting cases of
Table

1
; the reader is referred to
[
3
,
2
]
for details and examples.

Output:

The decomposition of
u_{i}!\langle V\rangle.Q
requires decomposing both the sent value
V
and the continuation
Q
.
We distinguish two cases, depending on the type
S
of
u_{i}
:

•

If
\neg\mathsf{tr}(S)
then
u_{i}
is linear or shared, and so the decomposition consists of a leading trio that
mimics an output action in parallel with the breakdown of
Q
.
The context
\widetilde{x}
must include the free variables of
V
and
Q
, which are
denoted
\widetilde{y}
and
\widetilde{w}
, respectively. These tuples are not
necessarily disjoint: variables with shared types can appear free in both
V

and
Q
. The value
V
is then broken down with parameters
\widetilde{y}
and
k+1
; the latter serves to consistently generate propagators for the
trios in the breakdown of
V
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\sigma}\big)}
.
The substitution
\sigma
increments the index of session
names; it is applied to both
V
and
Q
before they are broken down.
By taking
\sigma=\mathsf{next}(u_{i})
we
distinguish two cases:

–

If name
u_{i}
is linear (i.e., it has a session type) then its future
occurrences are renamed into
u_{i+1}
, and
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{i+1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{i}$}\}
;

–

Otherwise, if
u_{i}
is shared, then
\sigma=\{\}
.

Note that if
u_{i}
is linear then it appears either in
V
or
Q
and
\sigma

affects only one of them. The last prefix activates the
breakdown of
Q
with its corresponding context
\widetilde{w}
.

In case
V=y
, the same strategy applies;
notice that variable
y
is not propagated further if it does not appear free in
Q
.

•

If
\mathsf{tr}(S)
then
u_{i}
is tail-recursive, and so the decomposition consists of a leading trio that mimics the output action
running in parallel with the breakdown of
Q
. After receiving the context

\widetilde{x}
, the leading trio sends an abstraction
N_{V}
along
c^{u}
, which
performs several tasks. First,
N_{V}
collects the sequence of names
\tilde{u}
;
then, it mimics the output action of
P
along one of such names (denoted
u_{{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}}
, see below) and
triggers the next trio, with context
\widetilde{w}
; finally, it reinstates the
server on
c^{u}
for the next trio that uses
u
.
Indexing is not relevant in this case.

Input:

To decompose a process
u_{i}?(y).Q
we distinguish two cases, as before:
(i) name
u_{i}
is linear or shared or (ii) tail-recursive.
In case (i), the breakdown consists of a leading trio that mimics the input action and possibly extends the context with the
received variable
y
. In case (ii), when
u_{i}
has tail-recursive session type
S
, the decomposition is as in the output case.

Application:

For simplicity we consider the breakdown of applications of the form
V\,{(\widetilde{r},u_{i})}
, where
every
r_{i}\in\widetilde{r}
is such that
\mathsf{tr}(r_{i})
and only
u_{i}
is such that
\neg\mathsf{tr}(u_{i})
.
The general case involves different orders in names and multiple names with non-recursive types, and is defined similarly.

Before commenting on process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V\,{(\widetilde{r},u_{i})}}\big)}
,
let us discuss how names in
(\widetilde{r},u_{i})
are decomposed using types.
Letting
|\tilde{r}|=n
and
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
, for each
r_{i}\in\widetilde{r}
(with

r_{i}:S_{i}
) we generate a sequence
\widetilde{z}_{i}=(z^{i}_{1},\ldots,z^{i}_{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}|})
as in the output case.
We decompose name
u_{i}
(with
u_{i}:C
) as
\widetilde{m}=(u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}|-1})
.

The decomposition first receives a context
\widetilde{x}
for value
V
: we break down
V

with
\widetilde{x}
as a context since these variables need to be propagated to the
abstracted process.
Subsequently, an output on
c^{r_{1}}
sends a value containing
n
abstractions that occur nested
within output prefixes—as explained in
[
2
]
, this is similar to a partial instantiation mechanism.
For each
j\in\{1,\ldots,n-1\}
, each abstraction
binds
\widetilde{z}_{j}
and sends the next abstraction along
c^{r_{j+1}}
.
The innermost abstraction abstracts over
\widetilde{z}_{n}
and encloses
the process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\,{(\widetilde{z}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{n},\widetilde{m})}
, which effectively mimics the application.
This abstraction nesting binds all variables
\widetilde{z}_{i}
, the
decompositions of all tail-recursive names (
\widetilde{r}
).

Composition:

The breakdown of a process
Q\;|\;R
consists of a control trio that triggers the breakdowns of
Q
and
R
; it does not mimic any
action of the source process.
In the decomposed process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q\;|\;R}\big)}
, the tuple
\widetilde{y}\subseteq\widetilde{x}
(resp.
\widetilde{w}\subseteq\widetilde{x}
) collects the free variables in
Q
(resp.
R
). To avoid
name conflicts, the trigger for the breakdown of
R
is
\overline{c_{k+l+1}}
, with
l={{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}Q}\rbag}}
(cf.
Definition

2.5
).

Restriction:

To decompose
(\nu\,s:C)\,{P^{\prime}}
we examine
C
;
the interesting case is when
\mathsf{tr}(C)
.
In that case,
we decompose
s
into
\widetilde{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|})
and

\overline{s}
into
\widetilde{\overline{s}}=(\overline{s_{1}},\ldots,\overline{s_{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|}})
. Notice
that as
\mathsf{tr}(C)
we have

C\equiv\mu\mathsf{t}.S
, therefore
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}C})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
.
The breakdown introduces two servers in parallel with the breakdown of

P^{\prime}
; they provide names for
s
and
\overline{s}
along
c^{s}

and
c^{\overline{s}}
, respectively. The server on
c^{s}
(resp.

c^{\overline{s}}
) receives a value
and applies it to the sequence
\widetilde{s}
(resp.

\widetilde{\overline{s}}
). We restrict over
\widetilde{s}
and propagators

c^{s}
and
c^{\overline{s}}
.

Inaction:

To breakdown
\mathbf{0}
, we define a so-called
degenerate
trio with only one input prefix
that receives a context that by construction will always be empty (i.e.,
\widetilde{x}=\epsilon
).

Value:

Let us discuss the particular case of values of the form

\lambda(\widetilde{y},z):{(\widetilde{S},C)}^{\leadsto}.\,P
,
where
\mathsf{tr}(y_{i})
holds for every
y_{i}\in\widetilde{y}
and
\neg\mathsf{tr}(z)
, and

\leadsto\in\{\multimap,\rightarrow\}
.
The general case is defined similarly.

In the process for the decomposed value, denoted

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{V}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\lambda(\widetilde{y},z):{(\widetilde{S},C)}^{\leadsto}.\,P}\big)}
,
every
y_{i}
(with
y_{i}:S_{i}
) is
decomposed into
\widetilde{y}^{i}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S_{i}})}|})
. We use

C
to decompose
z
into
\widetilde{z}
.
We abstract over
\widetilde{y}^{1},\ldots,\widetilde{y}^{n},\widetilde{z}
;
the body of the abstraction (i.e.
N
) is the composition of recursive names
propagators, the control trio, and the breakdown of
P
, with name index
initialized with the substitution
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$z_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$z$}\}
.
For every
y_{i}\in\widetilde{y}
there is a server

c^{y_{i}}?(x).x\,{\widetilde{y}^{i}}

as a subprocess in the
abstracted composition—the rationale for these servers is as in previous cases.
We restrict the
propagators

\widetilde{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}}})
: this
enables us to type the value in a shared environment when
\leadsto=\rightarrow
.
Also, we restrict special propagator names
\widetilde{c}_{r}=\bigcup_{r\in\tilde{v}}c^{r}
.

Decomposing Types

We may now formally introduce the decompositions on types (and some associated notions), which have been informally used above.

Definition 2.6

(Decomposing Session Types)
.

Given the session, higher-order, and shared types of
Figure

5
, the
type decomposition function

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
is defined using the auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
as in
Figure

10
.
We write
|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|
to denote the length of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
(and similarly for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
).

Notice that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
is a partial function, which operates on a sub-class of non-tail-recursive session types: in
Figure

10
, the condition ‘
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}
is a singleton’ allows us to decompose types such as ‘
\mu\mathsf{t}.?((?(\mathsf{str}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathsf{str}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\mathsf{t})\!\rightarrow\!\diamond){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
’, which is non-tail-recursive and features sequencing as an argument but not at the top-level.

To handle the unfolding of recursive types, we shall use the following auxiliary function, which decomposes guarded recursive types, by first ignoring all the actions until the recursion.

Definition 2.7

(Decomposing an Unfolded Recursive Type)
.

Let
S
be a session type. The function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
: is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\mu\mathsf{t}.S})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}

Given an unfolded recursive session type
S
, the auxiliary function
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}
returns the position of the top-most prefix of
S
within its body.

Definition 2.8

(Index function)
.

Let
S
be an (unfolded) recursive session type. The function
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle}=\begin{cases}{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$S$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\}}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}&\text{if}\ S=\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}0}}}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l}}}=\begin{cases}|{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S})}|-l+1&\text{if $T=\mu\mathsf{t}.S$}\\
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}S}\rangle^{\star}_{{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}l+1}}}&\text{if $T=!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S$ or $T=?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S$}\end{cases}

Example 2.1
.

Let
S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}

and

T=?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
.
Then
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}T}\rangle}=2
since the top-most prefix of
T
(‘
?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}
’) is
the second prefix in the body of
S
.

The Process Decomposition and the Minimality Result.

Having introduced the breakdown function, we can now define the decomposition of
\mathsf{HO}
processes:

Definition 2.9

(Decomposing Processes
[
3
,
2
]
)
.

Let
P
be a closed
\mathsf{HO}
process such that
\widetilde{u}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
.
The
decomposition
of
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
, is
defined as:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,\big(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P\sigma}\big)}\big)

where:

k>0
;

\widetilde{c}=(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k+|P|-1})
;

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\tilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
;
and
the breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}
, is as defined in Table
1
.

As already mentioned, the
minimality result
in
[
3
]
ensures that if
P
is a well-typed
\mathsf{HO}
process then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
is a well-typed
\mu\mathsf{HO}
process. It attests that the sequentiality in the session types for
P
is appropriately accommodated by the decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}
. Its proof relies on an auxiliary result establishing the typability of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}
.

In the following, given a session environment
\Delta=\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}
, we shall write
\Delta_{1}\circ\Delta_{2}
to indicate the split of
\Delta
into an environment
\Delta_{1}
containing non-recursive names and a (disjoint) environment
\Delta_{2}
containing recursive names.
Also, we will use
\Theta,\Theta^{\prime},\ldots
to denote session environments induced by our decompositions.

Lemma 2.1

(Typability of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{\text{-}}_{\text{-}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}

[
3
]
)
.

Let
P
be an indexed
\mathsf{HO}
process and
V
be a value.

1.

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\circ\Delta_{\mu}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma_{1}})},\Phi;\emptyset;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})},\Theta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\big)}\triangleright\diamond
,
where:

•

k>0

•

\widetilde{r}=\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{\mu})

•

\Phi=\prod_{r\in\tilde{r}}c^{r}:\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{\mu}(r)})}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

•

\widetilde{x}=\mathsf{fv}(P)

•

\Gamma_{1}=\Gamma\setminus\widetilde{x}

•

\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)=\{c_{k},\ldots,c_{k+{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}}-1}\}\cup\{\overline{c_{k+1}},\ldots,\overline{c_{k+{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\lbag{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}\rbag}}-1}}\}

•

\Theta(c_{k})=?(U_{1},\ldots,U_{n})
, where
({\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})})(\widetilde{x})=(x_{1}:U_{1},\ldots,x_{n}:U_{n})

•

\mathsf{balanced}(\Theta)

2.

If
\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\circ\Delta_{\mu}\vdash V\triangleright\widetilde{T}\!\multimap\!\diamond
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma})},\Phi;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Lambda})};{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{V}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}V}\big)}\triangleright{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\widetilde{T}})}\!\multimap\!\diamond
,
where:

•

\widetilde{x}=\mathsf{fv}(V)

•

\Phi=\prod_{r\in\tilde{r}}c^{r}:\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{R}^{\star}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{\mu}(r)})}\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle

Theorem 2.2

(Minimality Result for
\mathsf{HO}

[
3
]
)
.

Let
P
be a closed
\mathsf{HO}
process (i.e.
\mathsf{fv}(P)=\emptyset
) with
\widetilde{u}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
.
If
\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta\circ\Delta_{\mu}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
,
then
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Gamma\sigma})};\emptyset;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta\sigma})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\Delta_{\mu}\sigma})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P})}\triangleright\diamond
, where
\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
.

Having summarized the results on which our developments stand (in particular, the minimality result for
\mathsf{HO}
), we now move on to establish the minimality result for
\pi
.

3
Decompose by Composing

In this section, our goal is to obtain a minimality result for
\pi
following the “decompose by composing” approach (cf.
Figure

1
).
We start by defining
\mu\pi
, i.e., the language
\pi
equipped with MSTs.
Then, we define a decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}:\pi\to\mu\pi
, given in terms of a breakdown function denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
(cf.
Table

2
).
As anticipated, this breakdown function will result from the composition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
, that is,

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}}\big)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
.
Using
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
, we shall obtain the desired minimality result for
\pi
, which will be given by
Theorem

3.1
.

Remark 3.1
.

In the following we focus on
\pi
, and so we sometimes use typing judgments of the form
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
, i.e., the judgment introduced in
Section

2.2
but without the environment
\Lambda
, which is not relevant for first-order processes.
Based on the rules in
Figure

6
, we can derive typing rules for
\pi
with polyadic communication.
Such rules, given in
Figure

11
, use the shortened typing judgments for
\pi
.

\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{}{\Gamma,\widetilde{x}:\widetilde{M}_{x};\widetilde{y}:\widetilde{M}_{y}\vdash\widetilde{x}\widetilde{y}\triangleright\widetilde{M}_{x}\widetilde{M}_{y}}\quad\textsc{(PolyVar)}\\[11.38109pt]
\frac{\Gamma;\Delta_{1},u:S\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447pt\Gamma;\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{x}\triangleright\widetilde{C}}{\Gamma;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2},u:!\langle\widetilde{C}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(PolySend)}\qquad\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\Delta_{1},u:S\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\Delta_{2}\vdash{\widetilde{x}}\triangleright{\widetilde{C}}\end{array}}{\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{x};\Delta_{1}\setminus\Delta_{2},u:?(\widetilde{C}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\vdash u?({\widetilde{x}}).P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(PolyRcv)}\\[11.38109pt]
\frac{\Gamma;\Delta,\widetilde{s}:\widetilde{S}_{1},\widetilde{\overline{s}}:\widetilde{S}_{2}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\hskip 8.19447ptS_{1}\ \mathsf{dual}\ S_{2}}{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,\widetilde{s})\,P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(PolyResS)}\qquad\frac{\Gamma;\Delta,\widetilde{a}:\widetilde{C}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma;\Delta\vdash(\nu\,\widetilde{a})\,P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(PolyRes)}\\[11.38109pt]
\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright\langle\widetilde{S}\rangle\quad\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{x}\triangleright\widetilde{S}\end{array}}{\Gamma;\Delta_{1},\Delta_{2}\vdash u!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Req)}\qquad\frac{\begin{array}[]{c}\Gamma;\emptyset\vdash u\triangleright\langle\widetilde{S}\rangle\quad\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad\Gamma;\Delta_{2}\vdash\widetilde{x}\triangleright\widetilde{S}\end{array}}{\Gamma\backslash\widetilde{x};\Delta_{1}\setminus\Delta_{2}\vdash u?(\widetilde{x}).P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Acc)}\end{array}

Figure 11:
Derived typing rules for the polyadic variant of
\pi
(cf.
Remark

3.1
)

3.1
Key Ideas

Conceptually, the breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
can be obtained in two steps:

1.

First, we use the composition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}}\big)}
, which given a
\pi
process returns a
\mu\mathsf{HO}
process.

2.

Second, we use
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
to transform the
\mu\mathsf{HO}
process obtained in (1) into a
\mu\pi
process.

We illustrate these two steps in detail, considering output, input, and recursive processes.

3.1.1
Output and Input Processes

Output.

Given
P=u_{i}!\langle w_{j}\rangle.{Q}
, we first obtain:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}u_{i}!\langle w_{j}\rangle.{Q}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}}\big)}=c_{k}?(\tilde{x}).u_{i}!\big\langle W\big\rangle.\overline{c_{k+3}}!\langle\tilde{x}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k+3}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}}\big)}

where

\displaystyle\sigma

\displaystyle=(u_{i}:S)\,\mathsf{::}\,\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{i+1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{i}$}\}\mathbf{:}\,\{\}

\displaystyle W

\displaystyle=\lambda z_{1}.\,\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{k+1}?().z_{1}?(x).\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle x\rangle\;|\;c_{k+2}?(x).\big(x\,{\widetilde{w}}\big)\big)

Then, we transform

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}u_{i}!\langle w_{j}\rangle.{Q}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}}\big)}

into the following
\mu\pi
process, using
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}u_{i}!\langle w_{j}\rangle.{Q}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}u_{i}!\langle w_{j}\rangle.{Q}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}}\big)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).(\nu\,a)\,\big(u_{i}!\langle a\rangle.\big(\overline{c_{k+3}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k+3}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}Q\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad a?(y).y?(z_{1}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle z_{1}\rangle\;|\;c_{k+1}?(z_{1}).z_{1}?(x).\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle x\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c_{k+2}?(x).(\nu\,s)\,\big(x!\langle s\rangle.\overline{s}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\big)\big)\big)

We briefly describe the resulting process.
The subprocess mimicking the output action on
u_{i}
is guarded by an input on
c_{k}
.
Then, the output of
w_{j}
on
u_{i}
is mimicked via several steps: first, a private name
a
is sent along
u_{i}
;
then, after several redirections involving local trios, the breakdown of
w_{j}

is sent along name
s
.
We can see that the output action on
u_{i}
enables the forwarding of the context
\widetilde{x}
to the breakdown of
Q
, the continuation of the output.

Another form of output is when both
u_{i}
and/or
w_{j}
have recursive types.
We shall refer to names with tail-recursive types as

recursive names
.
This output case with recursive names is similar to the one just discussed, and given in
Table

2
.

Input.

The breakdown of
u_{i}?(w).Q
is
as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}u_{i}?(w).Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}=

\displaystyle\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}u_{i}?(w).Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}}\big)}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\penalty\ c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).u_{i}?(y).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{x},y\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\quad(\nu\,s_{1})\,\big(c_{k+1}?(\widetilde{x},y).\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle y\rangle.\overline{c_{k+3}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad c_{k+2}?(y).(\nu\,s)\,\big(y!\langle s\rangle.\overline{s}!\langle s_{1}\rangle)\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad c_{k+3}?(\widetilde{x}).(\nu\,a)\,\big(\overline{s_{1}}!\langle a\rangle.\big(\overline{c_{k+l+4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{k+l+4}?().\mathbf{0}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad a?(y^{\prime}).y^{\prime}?(\widetilde{w}).\big(\overline{c_{k+4}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k+4}_{\widetilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$w_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$w$}\}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}\big)\big)\big)\big)

In this case, the activation on
c_{k}
enables the input on
u_{i}
.
After several redirections,
the actual input of variables
\widetilde{w}
is
on a name received for
y^{\prime}
, which binds them in the breakdown of
{Q}
.
Hence, the context
\widetilde{x}
does not get extended for an inductive call:
it only gets extended locally (it is propagated by
c_{k+1}
).
Indeed, the context is always empty and propagators only enable subsequent actions.
The context does play a role in breaking down input actions with recursive names: in that case, the variables
z_{X}
(generated in the encoding of recursion, cf.
Figure

8
) get propagated as context.

3.1.2
Recursion

Now, we illustrate the resulting decomposition for
processes involving tail-recursive names.

Output.

The breakdown of

r!\langle w_{j}\rangle.Q
when
\mathsf{tr}(r)
is as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}r!\langle w_{j}\rangle.Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}=

\displaystyle c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).(\nu\,a_{1})\,{}c^{r}!\langle a_{1}\rangle.\big({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k+3}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}\;|\;a_{1}?(y_{1}).y_{1}?(\widetilde{z}).W\big)

where:

\displaystyle\qquad W=(\nu\,a_{2})\,\big(z_{\iota(S)}!\langle a_{2}\rangle.\big(\overline{c_{k+3}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle.c^{r}?(b).(\nu\,s)\,\big(b!\langle s\rangle.\overline{s}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle\big)\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad a_{2}?(y_{2}).y_{2}?(z^{\prime}_{1}).\big(\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{k+1}?().z^{\prime}_{1}?(x).\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle x\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c_{k+2}?(x).(\nu\,s^{\prime})\,\big(x!\langle s^{\prime}\rangle.\overline{s}^{\prime}!\langle\widetilde{w}\rangle\big)\big)\big)\big)

This breakdown follows the essential ideas of
the case
\neg\mathsf{tr}(r)
, discussed above.
The difference is the following: before mimicking the action on
r
, the process has to obtain
the decomposition of name
r
by a communication on
c^{r}
.
Again, as a consequence of composing encodings, this communication is carried out via several channel redirections: first,
the fresh name
a_{1}
is sent along
c^{r}
; then,
a name is received on
a_{1}
, along which the breakdown of
r
, denoted by

\widetilde{z}
, is finally received.
Notice that here we obtain the entire decomposition of
r
.
However, to properly mimic the original
output action in
W
, we need one specific channel from
\widetilde{z}
, which is appropriately selected
based on its type
S
by the index function

\iota(\cdot)

given in
Definition

3.4
—this is the first action of
W
, i.e.,
z_{\iota(S)}
.
Finally, the entire decomposition of
r
is again made
available for future trios by communication on
c^{r}
.
This way, we are able to send back
recursive names so they can be used in the next instance of a recursive body.

Recursive process.

The process
\mu X.P
is broken down as given below. Recall that the mapping
|\!|\cdot|\!|

has been defined in
Definition

2.2
.

\displaystyle(\nu\,s_{1})\,\big(c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+3}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\quad c_{k+1}?(\widetilde{x}).(\nu\,a_{1})\,\big(\overline{s}_{1}!\langle a_{1}\rangle.\big(\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{k+2}?()\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c_{k+3}?(\widetilde{x}).s_{1}?(z_{x}).\overline{c_{k+4}}!\langle\widetilde{x},z_{x}\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k+4}_{\tilde{x},z_{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g,\{X\rightarrow{\tilde{n}}\}}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\ast\,a_{1}?(y^{\prime}_{1}).y^{\prime}_{1}?(|\!|\widetilde{n}^{1}|\!|,\ldots,|\!|\widetilde{n}^{m}|\!|,y_{1}).\widehat{P}\,\big)\big)\big)

where:

\displaystyle\widehat{P}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,\big(\prod_{0<i\leq m}c^{n_{i}}?(b).(\nu\,s^{\prime})\,\big(b!\langle s^{\prime}\rangle.\overline{s}^{\prime}!\langle|\!|\widetilde{n}^{i}|\!|\rangle\big)\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle\widetilde{x}\rangle\;|\;c_{k+2}?(\widetilde{x}).y_{1}?(z_{x}).\overline{c_{k+3}}!\langle\widetilde{x},z_{x}\rangle\;|\;\big\lfloor\!\!\big\lfloor{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k+3}_{\tilde{x},z_{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g,\{X\rightarrow{\tilde{n}}\}}\big\rfloor\!\!\big\rfloor_{\tilde{c},\tilde{c}_{r}}\big)

Moreover, above we assume that
\widetilde{n}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
,
m=|\widetilde{n}|
,
|\!|\widetilde{n}|\!|=(|\!|n_{1}|\!|,\dots,|\!|n_{m}|\!|)
,
|\!|n_{i}|\!|:S_{i}

and
|\!|\widetilde{n}^{i}|\!|=(|\!|n_{1}^{i}|\!|,\dots,|\!|n_{|{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}S_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}|}^{i}|\!|)

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,m\}
.

The breakdown of this process works in coordination with the breakdown of the recursive variable
X
(described below).
The main mechanism here is concerned with controlling
(i) the activation of
new instances of recursive body (i.e.,
P
) and
(ii) the propagation of recursive names to a subsequent instance
(i.e.,
\widetilde{n}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
).
The first instance is given by
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k+4}_{\tilde{x},z_{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g,\{X\rightarrow{\tilde{n}}\}}
. Notice that by the definition of
other cases, this instance will first collect all the
recursive names by the communication with top-level providers on
names
c^{r}
for
r\in\widetilde{n}
.
The mechanism for generating subsequent instances is given by the replicated process

\ast\,a_{1}?(y^{\prime}_{1}).y^{\prime}_{1}?(|\!|\widetilde{n}^{1}|\!|,\ldots,|\!|\widetilde{n}^{m}|\!|,y_{1}).\widehat{P}
.
Recall that replication is supported via the encoding
*P=\mu X.(P\;|\;X)
.
Here, the intention is that this process again receives
decompositions of recursive names in
\widetilde{n}
on shared name
a_{1}

from the breakdown of
X
: to see this,
notice that link to
a_{1}
is propagated to
the breakdown of
P
via name
z_{x}
.

Recursive Variable.

Following the previous description, the
variable
X
is broken down as follows:

\displaystyle(\nu\,s_{1})\,\big(c_{k}?(z_{x}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+2}}!\langle z_{x}\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\quad\qquad\qquad c_{k+2}?(z_{x}).\overline{s}_{1}!\langle z_{x}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+3}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{k+3}?()\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad c_{k+1}?(z_{x}).(\nu\,a_{1})\,\big(c^{n_{1}}!\langle a_{1}\rangle.\big(a_{1}?(y_{1}).y_{1}?(\widetilde{z}_{1}).\ldots(\nu\,a_{j})\,Q\big)\big)\big)

where:

\displaystyle\qquad Q=\big(c^{n_{j}}!\langle a_{j}\rangle.\big(a_{j}?(y_{j}).y_{j}?(\widetilde{z}_{j}).(\nu\,s^{\prime})\,\big(z_{x}!\langle s^{\prime}\rangle.\overline{s}^{\prime}!\langle\widetilde{z}_{1},\ldots,\widetilde{z}_{j},s_{1}\rangle\big)\big)\big)

and
\widetilde{n}=g(X)
,
|\widetilde{n}|=j
,

n_{i}:S
, and

\widetilde{z_{i}}=(z_{1}^{i},\dots,z_{|{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{R}_{\circ}(}S_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}|}^{i})

for
i\in\{1,\dots,j\}
.

As described above, the main role of this breakdown is to
send back decomposition of all recursive names used in a recursive body (given by

\widetilde{n}=g(X)
) to a next instance of a recursive body.
This breakdown accomplishes this by (i) first
collecting recursive names by communication on

c^{r}
for
r\in\widetilde{n}
with trios of a breakdown of the recursive body,
and (ii) the propagating those names to a subsequent instance
by communication on
z_{x}
.
As explained in the previous case,
this link is established at the entry of recursive process,
and propagated throughout trios of its decomposition up to this process.
