Definition 3.2

(Degree of a Process)
.

The
degree
of a process
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}
, is defined as:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}u_{i}!\langle w_{j}\rangle.Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+3

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}(\nu\,s:S)\,Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}\mathbf{0}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle=1

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}u_{i}?(x:C).Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+5

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}Q\;|\;R{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}R{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+1

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}X{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle=4

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}\mu X.Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+4

We define how to obtain MSTs for
\pi
from standard session types:

Definition 3.3

(Decomposing First-Order Types)
.

The decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
on finite types, obtained by
combining the mappings
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{1},{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{G}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2}
,
is defined in
Figure

13
(top, where omitted cases are
defined homomorphically).
It is extended to account for recursive session types in
Figure

13
(center).

The auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{R}_{\circ}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
, given in
Figure

13
(bottom), is used in
Table

2
to
decompose
guarded
tail-recursive types:
it skips session prefixes until a type of form
\mu\mathsf{t}.S

is encountered; when that occurs, the recursive type is decomposed using

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{R}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
.

We give the definition of the index function
for
\pi
recursive types by composing the index function
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\rangle}
(for
\mathsf{HO}
recursive types, cf.
Definition

2.8
)
and the encoding of types from
Figures

8
and
9
.
This composition is straightforward: notice that
{[{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\rangle}
counts prefixes
of session types, and that the encodings of types from
Figures

8
and
9

do not alter the number of prefixes.

Definition 3.4

(Index function)
.

Let
S
be an (unfolded) recursive session type. The function
\iota(S)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\iota(S)=\begin{cases}\widehat{\iota}_{0}(S^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$S$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\})&\text{if $S=\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}$}\\
\widehat{\iota}_{0}(S)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad\widehat{\iota}_{l}(T)=\begin{cases}\widehat{\iota}_{l+1}(S)&\text{if $T=!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S$ or $T=?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S$}\\
|{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{R}(}S{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}|-l+1&\text{if $T=\mu\mathsf{t}.S$}\end{cases}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}!\langle C\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{S}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}M&\text{if $S=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}$}\\
M,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}S{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where }M=!\big\langle{\langle?(?(\langle?({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}C{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle}\big\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}?(C){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{S}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}M&\text{if $S=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}$}\\
M,{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}S{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

\displaystyle\qquad\text{where }M=?(\langle?(?(\langle?({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}C{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}

Given a typed process
P
, we write
\mathsf{rn}(P)
to denote the set of free names of
P
whose types are recursive.
We are finally ready to define the decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
, the analog
of
Definition

2.9
but for processes in
\pi
:

Definition 3.5

(Process Decomposition)
.

Let
P
be a closed
\pi
process
with
\widetilde{u}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
and
\widetilde{v}=\mathsf{rn}(P)
.
Given the breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
in
Tables

2
and
3
, the decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
is defined as:

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,\Big(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}\;|\;\prod_{r\in\tilde{v}}c^{r}?(b).(\nu\,s)\,(b!\langle s\rangle.\overline{s}!\langle\widetilde{r}\rangle)\Big)

where:
k>0
,
\widetilde{c}=(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k+{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}-1})
;

\widetilde{c_{r}}=\bigcup_{r\in\tilde{v}}c^{r}
;

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\tilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
;
for each
r\in\tilde{v}
, we have
r:S
and
\widetilde{r}=r_{1},\ldots,r_{|{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}S{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}|}
.

3.3
Examples

We illustrate our constructions by means of two representative examples, involving delegation and recursive processes/types.

(A Recursive Process)
.

\displaystyle r_{1}:\mu\mathsf{t}.?\big({\langle?(?(\langle?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle}\big){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathsf{t}}

\displaystyle r_{2}:\mu\mathsf{t}.!\big\langle{\langle?(?(\langle?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle}\big\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathsf{t}}

\displaystyle(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,(\nu\,c^{r})\,\big(c^{r}?(b).(\nu\,s)\,\big(b!\langle s\rangle.\overline{s}!\langle r_{1},r_{2}\rangle\big)\;|\;\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{1}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$r_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{\emptyset}\big)

\widetilde{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}})
and

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{1}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$r_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{\emptyset}
is in
Figure

14
.

In
Figure

14
,
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{1}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$r_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$r$}\}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}
simulates
recursion in
P
using replication.
Given some index
k
, process
R^{k}
mimics actions of the recursive body.
It first gets a decomposition of
r
by interacting
with the process providing recursive names on
c^{r}
(for the first instance,
this is a top-level process in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
).
Then, it mimics the first input action on the
channel received for
z_{1}
(that is,
r_{1}
): the input of actual names for

w_{1}
is delegated through channel redirections to name
y_{2}
(both prefixes are highlighted
in
Figure

14
). Once the recursive name is used, the
decomposition of recursive name is made available for the
breakdown of the continuation by a communication on
c^{r}
.
Similarly, in the continuation, the second action on
r
, output, is mimicked by

r_{2}
(received for
z_{2}
), with the output of actual name
w_{1}
delegated to

\overline{s}^{\prime}
(both prefixes are highlighted in
Figure

14
).

Subprocess
R^{5}
is a breakdown of the first instance of the recursive body.
The replication guarded by
a_{1}
produces a next instance,
i.e.,
process
R^{2}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$x_{r_{1}},x_{r_{2}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$r_{1},r_{2}$}\}
in
\widehat{P}
. By
communication on
a_{1}
and a few reductions on propagators, it gets
activated: along
a_{1}
it first receives a name for
y^{\prime}_{1}
along which it
also receives: (i) recursive names
r_{1},r_{2}
for variables
x_{r_{1}},x_{r_{2}}
, and (ii) a name for
y_{1}
along which it will receive
a_{1}
again, for future
instances, as it can be seen in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k+7}_{z_{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}X{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
.

3.4
Results

We close this section by establishing the minimality result for
\pi
using the typability of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
. We need some auxiliary definitions to characterize
the propagators required to decompose recursive processes.

2.1
states typability results by
introducing two typing environments, denoted
\Theta
and
\Phi
.
While environment
\Theta
is used to type linear propagators (e.g.,
c_{k},c_{k+1},\ldots
) generated by the breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}
,
environment
\Phi
types shared propagators used in trios that propagate
breakdown of recursive names (e.g.,
c^{r},c^{v},\ldots
where
r
and
v
are
recursive names).

Definition 3.6

(Session environment for propagators)
.

Let
\Theta
be the session environment and
\Phi
be the recursive propagator
environment defined
in
2.1
. Then, by applying the encoding
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2}
, we define

\Theta^{\prime}
and
\Phi^{\prime}
as follows:
\Theta^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2},\Phi^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\Phi{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2}
.

We can use
\Theta^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\Theta{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2}
in the following statement, where we
state the typability result for the breakdown function.
The proof composes previously established results:

Lemma 3.1

(Typability of Breakdown)
.

Let P be an initialized
\pi
process. If
\Gamma;\Delta,\Delta_{\mu}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
, then

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})},\Phi^{\prime};{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})},\Theta^{\prime}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}\triangleright\diamond
,
where:

•

k>0;

•

\widetilde{r}=\mathsf{dom}(\Delta_{\mu})
;

•

\Phi^{\prime}=\prod_{r\in\tilde{r}}{c^{r}:\langle\langle?({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{R}_{\circ}(}\Delta_{\mu}(r){\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\rangle\rangle};

•

\mathsf{balanced}(\Theta^{\prime})
with

\mathsf{dom}(\Theta^{\prime})=\{c_{k},c_{k+1},\ldots,c_{k+{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}-1}\}\cup\{\overline{c_{k+1}},\ldots,\overline{c_{k+{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}{P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}-1}}\}

such that
\Theta^{\prime}(c_{k})=?(\cdot){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
.

Proof.

Directly from

2.1

and from Theorems 5.1 and 5.2 from
[
15
]
.
See
Section

A.2
for details.
∎

We now consider typability for the decomposition function, using

\Phi^{\prime}={\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}(\!\!\langle}\Phi{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rangle\!\!)}^{2}
as in
Definition

3.6
.

Theorem 3.1

(Minimality Result for
\pi
)
.

Let
P
be a closed
\pi
process, with
\widetilde{u}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
and
\widetilde{v}=\mathsf{rn}(P)
.
If
\Gamma;\Delta,\Delta_{\mu}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
, where
\Delta_{\mu}
only involves recursive session types, then

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Gamma\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})};{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Delta\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Delta_{\mu}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}\triangleright\diamond
, where

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
.

Proof.

Directly by using
3.1
;
see
Section

A.2
for details.
∎

While
Theorem

3.1
gives a useful
static
guarantee about the correctness of our decomposition of
P
into
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
, a
dynamic
guarantee that confirms that
P
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
are behaviorally equivalent is most relevant. Before establishing such a dynamic guarantee, we explore to what extent
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
can be optimized, i.e., whether redundancies induced by the “decompose by composing” approach can be safely eliminated.

4
An Optimized Decomposition

Although conceptually simple, the “decompose by composing” approach to the minimality result induces some suboptimal features. Considering this, in this section we propose
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, an optimization of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
with less synchronizations and direct support for recursion,
and establish its correctness properties, in terms of its corresponding minimality result (static correctness,
Theorem

4.1
, Page
4.1
) but also
dynamic correctness
(
Theorem

4.2
, Page
4.2
).

4.1
Motivation: Suboptimal Features of “Decompose by Composing”

To motivate our insights, consider the process
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}u_{i}?(w).{Q}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
as presented in
Section

3.1
and
Table

2
. We identify some
suboptimal features
of a
decomposition based on
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
:

Channel redirections

While the given process receives a name for variable
w
along
u_{i}
,
its breakdown does not input a breakdown of
w
directly, but does so through a series of
channel redirections:
u_{i}
receives a name along which
it sends the restricted name
s
, along which it sends the restricted name
s_{1}

and so on. Finally, the name received for
y^{\prime}
receives
\widetilde{w}
, the breakdown of
w
.
This redundancy is perhaps more evident in
Definition

3.3
, which gives the translation of types by composition:
the mimicked input action is five-level nested for
the original name.
This resulting type is due to the composition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
.

Redundant synchronizations on propagators

Also,
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}u_{i}?(w).{Q}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
features redundant communications on propagators.
For example, the bound name
y
is locally propagated
by
c_{k+1}
and
c_{k+2}
.
This is the result of breaking down sequential prefixes induced by

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
(not present in the original process).

Trio structure is lost

Last but not least, the trio structure is lost as
subprocess
\widehat{Q}_{\tilde{x}}
is guarded and nested, and
it
inductively invokes the function on continuation
Q
.
This results in an arbitrary level of process nesting, which is induced by the final application of encoding

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}
in the composition.

The shortcomings of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}
are also evident in the treatment of recursive processes and names.
Because
\mathsf{HO}
does not feature recursion constructs,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}
encodes them by relying on abstraction passing
and shared abstractions.
Then, going back to
\pi
via
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}_{g}
,
these representations are translated to a process involving
a replicated subprocess.
However, going through this path the encoding of recursive process becomes convoluted. Moreover, all non-optimal features already discussed for the case of input are also present in the decomposition of recursion.

Based on these observations, here we develop an optimized decomposition function, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
(
Definition

4.8
),
that avoids the shortcomings described above.
The optimized decomposition relies on a streamlined breakdown function, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
, which produces a composition of trios processes, with a fixed maximum number of nested prefixes.
The decomposed process avoids channel redirections and only introduces propagators that codify the sequentiality of the original process.

Roadmap.

To facilitate reading, we summarize notations and definitions related to
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
and their corresponding notions for the optimized decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
—see
Table

4
.

Section

3

P

(

)

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}
(

)

and

(

)

and

and

)

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}

4.2
Preliminaries

As before, we need to decompose a session type into a
list
of minimal session types:

Definition 4.1

(Decomposing Types)
.

Let
S
and
C
be a session and a channel type, resp.
(cf.
Figure

5
).
The
type decomposition
function

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
is defined in
Figure

15
.

As before, we need two auxiliary functions for decomposing recursive types, denoted

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.

A comparison between
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
(
Figure

13
) is already useful to understand the intent and scope of the optimized decomposition. Consider, e.g., the decompositions of the session type
!\langle C\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{S}
(with
S\neq\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
):

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}!\langle C\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{S}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}

\displaystyle=!\big\langle{\langle?(?(\langle?({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}C{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\rangle}\big\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}S{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}!\langle C\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{S}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

\displaystyle=!\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}{\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}\,,{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

These differences at the level of induced MSTs will be useful in our formal comparison of the two decompositions, in
Section

4.5
.

Example 4.1

(Decomposing a Recursive Type)
.

Let

S=\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}
be a recursive session type, with
S^{\prime}=?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
.
By
Figure

15
,
since
S
is tail-recursive,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.
Further,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathsf{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

By definition of

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, we obtain

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}t{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

(using
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}
). Since

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}\mathsf{t}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\epsilon
, we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}

Example 4.2

(Decomposing an Unfolded Recursive Type)
.

Let
T=?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S
be a derived unfolding of
S
from
Example

4.1
. Then, by
Figure

15
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}T{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
is the list of minimal recursive
types obtained as follows: first,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}T{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
and after one more step,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
. Finally, we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}S^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.
We get the same list of minimal types as in
Example

4.1
:

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}_{\circ}(}T{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t},\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}

Definition 4.2

(Decomposing Environments)
.

Given environments
\Gamma
and
\Delta
, we define
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
inductively as
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\emptyset{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\emptyset
and

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta,u_{i}:S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})},(u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|-1}):{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma,u_{i}:\langle S\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})},u_{i}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\langle S\rangle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

We now define the (optimized) degree of a process. A comparison with the previous definition (
Definition

3.2
) provides further indication of the improvements induced by optimized decomposition.

Definition 4.3

(Degree of a Process)
.

The
optimized degree
of a process
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}
, is inductively defined as follows:

\begin{cases}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+1&\text{if $P=u_{i}!\langle y\rangle.Q$ or $P=u_{i}?(y).Q$}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}&\text{if $P=(\nu\,s:S)\,Q$}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+1&\text{if $P=(\nu\,r:S)\,Q$ and $\mathsf{tr}(S)$}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+1&\text{if $P=Q\;|\;R$}\\
1&\text{if $P=\mathbf{0}$ or $P=X$}\\
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+1&\text{if }P=\mu X.Q\end{cases}

As before, given a finite tuple of names

\widetilde{u}=(a,b,s,s^{\prime},\ldots)
, we write
\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})
to denote the tuple

(a_{1},b_{1},s_{1},s^{\prime}_{1},\ldots)
; recall that a process is initialized if all of its names are indexed.

Given two tuples of indexed names
\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{x}
, it is useful to
collect those names in
\widetilde{x}
that appear
in
\widetilde{u}
.

Definition 4.4

(Free indexed names)
.

Let
\widetilde{u}
and
\widetilde{x}
be two tuples
of names. We define the set

\mathsf{fnb}(\widetilde{u},\widetilde{x})
as
\{z_{k}:z_{i}\in\widetilde{u}\wedge z_{k}\in\widetilde{x}\}
.

As usual, we treat sets of names as tuples (and vice-versa). By abusing
notation, given a process
P
, we shall write
\mathsf{fnb}(P,\widetilde{y})
to stand for

\mathsf{fnb}(\mathsf{fn}(P),\widetilde{y})
. Then, we have that
\mathsf{fnb}(P,\widetilde{x})\subseteq\widetilde{x}
. In the definition of the breakdown function, this notion allows
us to conveniently determine a
context
for a subsequent trio.

Definition 4.5
.

Given a process
P
, we write
\mathsf{frv}(P)

to denote that
P
has a free recursive variable.

Remark 4.1
.

Whenever

c_{k}?(\widetilde{y})
(resp.
\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{y}\rangle
) with
\widetilde{y}=\epsilon
,
we shall write
c_{k}?()
(resp.
\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle
)
to stand for
c_{k}?(y)
(resp.
\overline{c_{k}}!\langle y\rangle
) such that
c_{k}:?(\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\rangle){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
(resp.
\overline{c_{k}}:!\langle\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\rangle\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
).

Definition 4.6

(Index function)
.

Let
S
be an (unfolded) recursive session type. The function
\iota(S)
is defined as follows:

\displaystyle\iota(S)=\begin{cases}\widehat{\iota}_{0}(S^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$S$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\})&\text{if $S=\mu\mathsf{t}.S^{\prime}$}\\
\widehat{\iota}_{0}(S)&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}\qquad\widehat{\iota}_{l}(T)=\begin{cases}\widehat{\iota}_{l+1}(S)&\text{if $T=!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S$ or $T=?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S$}\\
|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|-l+1&\text{if $T=\mu\mathsf{t}.S$}\end{cases}

Definition 4.7

(Name breakdown)
.

Let
u_{i}:C
be an indexed name with its session type.
We write
\mathsf{nbd}(u_{i}:C)
to denote

\mathsf{nbd}(u_{i}:C)=(u_{i},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|-1})

We extend
\mathsf{nbd}(\cdot)
to work on lists of assignments (name, type), as follows:

\mathsf{nbd}((u^{1}_{i},\ldots,u^{n}_{j}):(C_{1},\ldots,C_{n}))=\mathsf{nbd}(u^{1}_{i}:C_{1})\cdot\ldots\cdot\mathsf{nbd}(u^{n}_{j}:C_{n})

Table 6:
The auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
.

We define the optimized decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
by relying on the revised breakdown function

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
(cf.
Section

4.3.1
).
Given a context
\widetilde{x}
and a
k>0
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
is defined
on initialized processes.

Table

5
gives the definition: we use an auxiliary function for
recursive processes, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
, where
parameter
g
maps recursive variables
to a list of name variables (cf.
Section

4.3.2
).

In the following, to keep presentation simple, we assume recursive processes
\mu X.P
in which
P
does not contain a subprocess of shape
\mu Y.P^{\prime}
. The generalization of our decomposition without this assumption is not difficult, but is notationally heavy.

4.3.1
The Optimized Breakdown Function

We describe entries 1-7 in
Table

5
, assuming the side conditions given in the table.

1. Input

Process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}u_{i}?(y).Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

consists of a leading trio that
mimics the input and runs in parallel with the
breakdown of
Q
.
In the trio, a context
\widetilde{x}
is expected along
c_{k}
.
Then, an input on
u_{l}
mimics the input action:
it expects the
decomposition
of name
y
, denoted
\widetilde{y}
.
To decompose
y
we use its type: if
y:S
then

\widetilde{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|})
.
The index
l
of
u_{l}
depends on the type of
u_{i}
.
Intuitively, if
u_{i}
is tail-recursive then
l=\iota(S)
(
Definition

4.6
) and we do not increment it, as the same decomposition of
u_{i}
should be used to mimic a new instance in the continuation.
Otherwise, if
u_{i}
is linear then we use the substitution

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{i+1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{i}$}\}
to increment the index in
Q
.
Next, the context
\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fnb}(Q,\widetilde{x}\widetilde{y}\setminus\widetilde{w})
is propagated, where
\widetilde{w}=(u_{i})
or
\widetilde{w}=\epsilon
.

2. Output

Process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}u_{i}!\langle y_{j}\rangle.Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
sends the decomposition of
y
on
u_{l}
, with
l
as in the input case.
We decompose name
y_{j}
based on its type
S
:

\widetilde{y}=(y_{j},\ldots,y_{j+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|-1})
.
The context to be propagated is
\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fnb}(P,\widetilde{x}\setminus\widetilde{w})
,
where
\widetilde{w}
and
\sigma
are as in the input case.

3. Restriction (Non-recursive name)

The breakdown of process
(\nu\,s:C)\,{Q}
is

(\nu\,\widetilde{s}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})\,{\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}}

where
s
is decomposed using
C
:

\widetilde{s}=(s_{1},\ldots,s_{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|})
.
Since
(\nu\,s)\,
binds
s
and
\overline{s}

(or only
s
if
C
is a shared type)
the substitution
\sigma
is simply
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s_{1}\overline{s}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$s\overline{s}$}\}

and initializes indexes in
Q
.

4. Restriction (Recursive name)

As in the previous case, in the breakdown of
(\nu\,s:\mu\mathsf{t}.S)\,{Q}
the name
s
is decomposed into
\widetilde{s}
by relying on
\mu\mathsf{t}.S
.
Here the breakdown consists of the breakdown of
Q

running in parallel with a control trio, which appends
restricted (recursive) names
\widetilde{s}
and
\widetilde{\overline{s}}

to the context, i.e.,
\widetilde{z}=\widetilde{x},\widetilde{s},\widetilde{\overline{s}}
.

5. Composition

The breakdown of process
Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}
uses
a control trio to trigger the breakdowns of
Q_{1}
and
Q_{2}
, similarly as before.

6. Inaction

The breakdown of
\mathbf{0}
is an input prefix that receives an empty context (
\widetilde{x}=\epsilon
).

7. Recursion

The breakdown of
\mu X.P
is as follows:

\displaystyle(\nu\,c^{r}_{X})\,(c_{k}?(\widetilde{x}).\overline{c^{r}_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rangle.\mu X.c^{r}_{X}?(\widetilde{y}).\overline{c^{r}_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{y}\rangle.X\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k+1}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g})

We have a control trio and the breakdown of
P
, obtained using the auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
(
Table

6
). The trio receives the context
\widetilde{x}
on
c_{k}
and
propagates it further.
To ensure typability, we bind all session free names of
P
using the context
\widetilde{z}
, which contains the decomposition of those free names (cf.
Definition

4.7
). This context is needed to break down
P
, and so we record it as
g=\{X\mapsto\widetilde{z}\}
in the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k+1}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
. This way,
\widetilde{z}
will be propagated all the way until reaching
X
.

Next, the recursive trio is enabled, and receives
\widetilde{y}
along
c^{r}_{X}
, with
|\widetilde{z}|=|\widetilde{y}|
and
l=|P|
.
The tuple
\widetilde{y}
is propagated to the first trio of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k+1}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
.
By definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k+1}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
, its propagator

c^{r}_{X}
will send the same context as received by the first
trio. Hence, the recursive part of the control
trio keeps sending this context to the next instances of
recursive trios of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k+1}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
.

Notice that the leading trio actually has four prefixes. This simplifies our presentation: this trio can be broken down into two trios by introducing an extra propagator
c_{k+1}
to send over
c^{r}_{k+2}
.

4.3.2
Handling
P
in
\mu X.P

As already mentioned, we use the auxiliary function

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
in
Table

6
to generate
recursive
trios
.
We concentrate on discussing entries 8-11 in
Table

6
; the other entries are similar as before.
A key observation is that parameter
g
can be empty.
To see this, consider a process like
P=\mu X.(Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2})

where
X
occurs free in
Q_{1}
but not in
Q_{2}
.
If
X
occurs free in
Q_{1}
then its decomposition will have a non-empty

g
, whereas the
g
for
Q_{2}
will be empty.
In the recursive trios of
Table

6
, the difference between
g\not=\emptyset
and
g=\emptyset
is subtle: in the former case,
X
appears guarded by a propagator; in the latter case, it appears unguarded in a parallel composition.
This way, trios in the breakdown of
Q_{2}
replicate themselves on a trigger
from the breakdown of
Q_{1}
.

Having discussed this difference, we only describe the cases when
g\not=\emptyset
:

8 / 9. Output and Input

The breakdown of
u_{i}!\langle y_{j}\rangle.{Q}
consists of the breakdown of
Q
in parallel with a leading trio, a recursive process whose body is defined as in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{B}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}\big)}
.
As names
g(X)
may not appear free in
{Q}
,
we must ensure that a context
\widetilde{z}
for
the recursive body is propagated.
The breakdown of
r?(y).{Q}
is defined similarly.

10. Parallel Composition

We discuss the breakdown of
Q_{1}\;|\;Q_{2}
assuming
\mathsf{frv}(Q_{1})
. We take
\widetilde{y}_{1}=g(X)\cup\mathsf{fnb}(Q_{1},\widetilde{x})

to ensure that
g(X)
is propagated to the breakdown of
X
.
The role of
c^{r}_{k+l+1}
is to enact a new instance
of the breakdown of
Q_{2}
; it has a shared type to enable replication.
In a running process, the number of these triggers in parallel
denotes the number of available instances of
Q_{2}
.

11. Recursive Variable

In this case, the breakdown is a control trio
that receives the context
\widetilde{x}
from a preceding trio
and propagates it again to the first
control trio of the breakdown of
a recursive process along
c^{r}_{X}
.
Notice that by construction we have

\widetilde{x}=g(X)
.

We may now define the optimized process decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
:

Definition 4.8

(Decomposing Processes, Optimized)
.

Let
P
be a process with
\widetilde{u}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
and
\widetilde{v}=\mathsf{rn}(P)
.
Given the breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
in
Table

5
, the
optimized decomposition
of
P
, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, is
defined as

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,\big(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{r}\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{r}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\big)

where:
k>0
;

\widetilde{c}=(c_{k},\ldots,c_{k+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}-1})
;

\widetilde{r}
such that for
v\in\widetilde{v}
and
v:S

(v_{1},\ldots,v_{|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{R}^{*}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|})\subseteq\widetilde{r}
; and

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
.

The definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
is similar to the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
given in
Definition

3.5
. The optimizations are internal to the definition of the breakdown function; notice that the handling of recursive names needed in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
is not needed in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, as they are now handled by the auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
.

4.4
Examples

Here we illustrate

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
,

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
, and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
by revisiting the two examples from
Section

3
.

Example 4.3

(
Example

3.1
, Revisited)
.

Consider again the process
P=(\nu\,u)\,(A\;|\;B)
as in
Example

3.1
.
Recall that
P
implements session types
S=!\langle\overline{T}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}

and
T=?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}bool}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
.

By
Definition

4.3
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}=9
.
The optimized decomposition of
P
is:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,\big(\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;(\nu\,u_{1})\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{1}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}(A\;|\;B)\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\big)\quad

where
\sigma^{\prime}=\mathsf{init}(\mathsf{fn}(P))\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{1}\overline{u_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u\overline{u}$}\}

and
\widetilde{c}=(c_{1},\ldots,c_{9})
.
We have:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{1}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}(A\;|\;B)\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\big)

\displaystyle=c_{1}?().\overline{c_{2}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{2}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}A\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{6}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}B\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

The breakdowns of sub-processes
A
and
B
are as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{2}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}A\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{2}?().u_{1}!\langle w_{1},w_{2}\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{3}?().\overline{w}_{1}?(t).\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{4}?().\overline{w}_{2}!\langle\textsf{odd}(t)\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{6}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}B\sigma^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{1}?(x_{1},x_{2}).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle x_{1},x_{2}\rangle\!\;|\;\!c_{7}?(x_{1},x_{2}).x_{1}!\langle\textsf{5}\rangle.\overline{c_{8}}!\langle x_{2}\rangle\!\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{8}?(x_{2}).x_{2}?(b_{1}).\overline{c_{9}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{9}?().\mathbf{0}

Name
w
is decomposed as indexed names

\overline{w}_{1},\overline{w}_{2}
; by using
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
(
Definition

4.1
) on
T
, their MSTs are
M_{1}=!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
and

Name
u_{1}

We discuss the reduction steps from
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

After a few administrative reductions on

and

and the communication along
