\widetilde{c}_{**}=(c_{4},c_{5},c_{8},c_{9})
.
Further reductions follow similarly.

Example 4.4

(
Example

3.2
, Revisited)
.

Consider again the tail-recursive session type
S=\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
.
Also, let
R
be a process implementing a channel
r
with type
S
:

\displaystyle R=\mu X.r?(z).r!\langle-z\rangle.X

We decompose name
r
using
S
and obtain two channels
typed with MSTs as in
Figure

15
. We have:

r_{1}:\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
and

r_{2}:\mu\mathsf{t}.!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}int}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}
.

The trios produced by
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

satisfy two properties: they
(1) mimic the recursive behavior of
R

and (2) use the same decomposition of channel
r
(i.e.,
r_{1}
,
r_{2}
) in every instance.

To accomplish (1), each trio of the breakdown of the recursion body
is a recursive trio.
For (2), we need two things.
First, we expect to receive all recursive names in the
context
\widetilde{x}
when entering the decomposition of the recursion body;
further,
each trio should use one recursive name from the names
received and propagate

all
of them to subsequent trio. Second, we need an
extra control trio when breaking down prefix
\mu X
: this trio
(i) receives recursive names from the
last trio in the breakdown of the recursion body and
(ii) activates another instance with these recursive names.

Using these ideas, process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{1}_{r_{1},r_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
is as follows (we write
R^{\prime}
to stand for
r?(z).r!\langle-z\rangle.X
):

\displaystyle c_{1}?(r_{1},r_{2}).\overline{c^{r}_{2}}!\langle r_{1},r_{2}\rangle.\mu X.c^{r}_{X}?(y_{1},y_{2}).\overline{c^{r}_{2}}!\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle.X\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,2}_{r_{1},r_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

where
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,2}_{r_{1},r_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
is the composition of three recursive trios:

\displaystyle\mu X.c^{r}_{2}?(y_{1},y_{2}).r_{1}?(z_{1}).\overline{c^{r}_{3}}!\langle y_{1},y_{2},z_{1}\rangle.X\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\mu X.c^{r}_{3}?(y_{1},y_{2},z_{1}).r_{2}?(-z_{1}).\overline{c^{r}_{4}}!\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle.X\;|\;\mu X.c^{r}_{4}?(y_{1},y_{2}).\overline{c^{r}_{X}}!\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle.X

c^{r}_{2}
will first
activate the recursive trios
with context
(r_{1},r_{2})
.
Next,
each trio uses one of
r_{1},r_{2}
and propagate them both
mimicking the recursion body.
The last recursive trio
sends
r_{1},r_{2}
back to the top-level control trio, so it
can enact another instance
of the decomposition of the recursion body by activating the
first recursive trio.

4.5
Measuring the Optimization

Before discussing the static and dynamic correctness of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, here we measure the improvements over
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
.
A key metric for comparison is the number of prefixes/sychronizations induced by each decomposition. This includes (1) the number of prefixes involved in
channel redirections
and (2) the
number of propagators
; both can be counted by already defined notions:

1.

Channel redirections can be
counted by the levels of nesting in the decompositions of types (cf.
Figures

13
and
15
).

2.

The number of propagators is determined by the degree of a process
(cf.
Definitions

3.2
and
4.3
).

These two metrics are related; let us discuss them in detail.

Channel redirections.

The decompositions of types for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
abstractly describe the respective channel redirections.
The type decomposition for
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
(
Figure

13
) defines 5 levels of nesting for the translation of input/output types. Then, at the level of (decomposed) processes, channels with these types implement redirections: the nesting levels correspond to 5 additional prefixes in the decomposed process that mimic a source input/output action. In contrast, the type decomposition for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
(
Figure

15
) induces no nesting, and so at the level of processes there are no additional prefixes.

Number of propagators.

We define auxiliary functions to count the number of propagators induced by

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
. These functions, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, respectively, are defined using the degree functions
(
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}
) given by
Definitions

3.2
and
4.3
, respectively.

Remarkably,
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}
and

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
are not equal.
The difference lies in the
number of tail-recursive names in a process. In
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}

there are propagators
c_{k}
but also propagators
c^{r}
for recursive
names.

Definition

3.2
, however, only counts the former kind of propagators.
For any
P
, the number of
propagators
c^{r}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
is the number of free and bound
tail-recursive names in
P
. We remark that, by definition, there may be more than one occurrence of a propagator
c^{r}
in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
: there is at least one prefix with subject
c^{r}
; further occurrences depend on the sequentiality structure of the (recursive) type assigned to
r
.
On the other hand, in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
there are propagators

c_{k}
and
propagators
c^{r}_{X}
,
whose number corresponds
to the number of recursive variables in the process.
To define

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
and

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
,
we write
\mathsf{brn}(P)
to denote bound occurrences of recursive names
and
\#_{X}(P)
to denote the number of occurrences of recursive variables.

Definition 4.9

(Propagators in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
)
.

Given a process
P
, the number of propagators in each decomposition is given by

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P)|+|\mathsf{rn}(P)|\qquad{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+\#_{X}(P)

Notice that

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
gives the exact number of actions induced by propagators in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
; in contrast, due to propagators
c^{r}
,

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
gives the
least
number of such actions in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
.

In general, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}\geq{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, but we can be more precise for a broad class of processes.
We say that a process
P\not\equiv\mathbf{0}

is in
normal form
if
P=(\nu\,\widetilde{n})\,(Q_{1}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;Q_{n})
, where each
Q_{i}
(with
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
) is not
\mathbf{0}
and does not contain restriction nor parallel composition at the top-level.
We have the following result; see

Section

A.3
for details.

Lemma 4.1
.

If
P
is in normal form
then
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.

This result implies that the number of (extra) synchronizations induced by propagators in
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
is larger than in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.

4.6
Static Correctness

Here we establish the analog of

Theorem

3.1

(
Section

3.4
) but for
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.
We rely on an auxiliary predicate:

Definition 4.10

(Indexed Names)
.

Suppose some typing environments
\Gamma,\Delta
.
Let
\widetilde{x}
,
\widetilde{y}
be two tuples of indexed names.
We write
\mathsf{indexed}_{\Gamma,\Delta}(\widetilde{y},\widetilde{x})
for the predicate

\forall z_{i}.\left(z_{i}\in\widetilde{x}\Leftrightarrow\exists m.((z_{i},\ldots,z_{i+m-1})\subseteq\widetilde{y}\wedge m=|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}(\Gamma,\Delta)(z_{i}){\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|)\right)

We now state our static correctness results (typability with respect to MSTs) for the auxiliary function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
, the breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
, and the optimized decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
:

Lemma 4.2

(Typability of Auxiliary Breakdown:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}
)
.

Let
P
be an initialized process.
If
\Gamma\cdot X:\Delta_{\mu};\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

then:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma\setminus\widetilde{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})};\Theta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{g}\triangleright\diamond\quad(k>0)

where

•

\widetilde{x}\subseteq\mathsf{fn}(P)

such that

\Delta\setminus\widetilde{x}=\emptyset
;

•

\widetilde{y}=\widetilde{v}\cdot\widetilde{m}
, where

\widetilde{m}=\mathsf{codom}(g)
and

\widetilde{v}
is such that

\mathsf{indexed}_{\Gamma,\Delta}(\widetilde{v},\widetilde{x})

holds;

•

\Theta=\Theta_{\mu},\Theta_{X}(g)

where

–

\mathsf{dom}(\Theta_{\mu})=\{c^{r}_{k},c^{r}_{k+1},\ldots,c^{r}_{k+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}-1}\}\cup\{\overline{c^{r}_{k+1}},\ldots,\overline{c^{r}_{k+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}-1}}\}

–

Let
\widetilde{N}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta_{\mu}\cdot\Delta{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})(\widetilde{y})
. Then

\Theta_{\mu}(c^{r}_{k})=\begin{cases}\mu\mathsf{t}.?(\widetilde{N}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathsf{t}&\text{if $g\not=\emptyset$}\\
\langle\widetilde{N}\rangle&\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

–

\mathsf{balanced}(\Theta_{\mu})

–

\Theta_{X}(g)=\bigcup_{X\in\mathsf{dom}(g)}c^{r}_{X}:\langle\widetilde{M}_{X}\rangle

where

\widetilde{M}_{X}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta_{\mu}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})(g(X))
.

Proof.

By induction on the structure of
P
; see
A.5
for details.
∎

Lemma 4.3

(Typability of Breakdown)
.

Let
P
be an initialized process.
If
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma\setminus\widetilde{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta\setminus\widetilde{x}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})},\Theta\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\triangleright\diamond\quad(k>0)

where

•

\widetilde{x}\subseteq\mathsf{fn}(P)
and
\widetilde{y}

such that
\mathsf{indexed}_{\Gamma,\Delta}(\widetilde{y},\widetilde{x})
holds.

•

\mathsf{dom}(\Theta)=\{c_{k},c_{k+1},\ldots,c_{k+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}-1}\}\cup\{\overline{c_{k+1}},\ldots,\overline{c_{k+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}-1}}\}

•

\Theta(c_{k})=?(\widetilde{M}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}
,
where
\widetilde{M}=({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})},{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})(\widetilde{y})
.

•

\mathsf{balanced}(\Theta)

Proof.

By induction of the structure of
P
and
Lemma

4.2
;
see
Section

A.4
for details.
∎

We now (re)state the minimality result, now based on the decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.

Theorem 4.1

(Minimality Result for
\pi
, Optimized)
.

Let
P
be a process with
\widetilde{u}=\mathsf{fn}(P)
.
If
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
then

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\triangleright\diamond
, where

\sigma=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathsf{init}(\widetilde{u})$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{u}$}\}
.

Proof.

Direct by
Definition

4.8
and
Lemma

4.3
;
see
Section

A.5
for details.
∎

4.7
Dynamic Correctness

As a complement to the minimality result just given, here we establish that
P
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
are
behaviorally equivalent
(
Theorem

4.2
).
The notion of behavioral equivalence that we consider is
MST-bisimilarity
(cf.
Definition

4.20
,
\approx^{\mathtt{M}}
), a variant of
characteristic bisimilarity
, one of the session-typed behavioral equivalences studied in
[
14
]
.

4.7.1
Preliminaries

We require some auxiliary definitions and notations from
[
14
]
.

Typed Labeled Transition System.

Our typed LTS is obtained by coupling the untyped LTS given in
Figure

4

with a labeled transition relation
on typing environments, given in
Figure

16
.
Building upon the reduction relation for session environments in
Definition

2.1
,
such a relation
is defined on triples of environments by
extending the LTSs
in
[
17
,
16
]
; it is
denoted

(\Gamma_{1},\Lambda_{1},\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}(\Gamma_{2},\Lambda_{2},\Delta_{2})

Recall that
\Gamma
admits weakening.
Using this principle (not valid for
\Lambda
and
\Delta
), we have

(\Gamma^{\prime},\Lambda_{1},\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xmapsto{\ell}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\Lambda_{2},\Delta_{2})

whenever

(\Gamma,\Lambda_{1},\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xmapsto{\ell}}(\Gamma^{\prime},\Lambda_{2},\Delta_{2})
.
Some intuitions follow.

Input Actions

are defined by
Rules
{\footnotesize[\textsc{SRv}]}
and
{\footnotesize[\textsc{ShRv}]}
.
In Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{SRv}]}

the type of value
V

and the type of the object associated to the session type on
s

should coincide.
The resulting type tuple must contain the environments
associated to
V
.
The
dual endpoint
\overline{s}
cannot be
present in the session environment: if it were present
the only possible communication would be the interaction
between the two endpoints (cf. Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{Tau}]}
).
Following similar principles,
Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{ShRv}]}
defines input actions for shared names.

Output Actions

are defined by Rules
{\footnotesize[\textsc{SSnd}]}

and
{\footnotesize[\textsc{ShSnd}]}
.
Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{SSnd}]}
states the conditions for observing action

(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,s!\langle V\rangle
on a type tuple

(\Gamma,\Lambda,\Delta\cdot s\!:\!S)
.
The session environment
\Delta\,,\,s\!:\!S

should include the session environment of the sent value
V
(denoted
\Delta^{\prime}
in the rule),

excluding
the session environments of names
m_{j}

in
\widetilde{m}
which restrict the scope of value
V
(denoted
\Delta_{j}
in the rule).
Analogously, the linear variable environment

\Lambda^{\prime}
of
V
should be included in
\Lambda
.
The rule defines the scope extrusion of session names in
\widetilde{m}
; consequently,
environments associated to
their dual endpoints (denoted
\Delta^{\prime}_{j}
in the rule) appear in
the resulting session environment. Similarly for shared
names in
\widetilde{m}
that are extruded.
All free values used for typing
V
(denoted
\Lambda^{\prime}
and
\Delta^{\prime}
in the rule) are subtracted from the
resulting type tuple. The prefix of session
s
is consumed
by the action.
Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{ShSnd}]}
follows similar ideas for output actions on shared names:
the name must be typed with
\langle U\rangle
;
conditions on value
V
are identical to those on Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{SSnd}]}
.

Other Actions

Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{Tau}]}
defines
internal transitions:
it reduces the session environment (cf.
Definition

2.1
) or keeps it
unchanged.

We illustrate Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{SSnd}]}
by means of an example:

Example 4.5
.

Consider the environment tuple

(\Gamma;\,\emptyset;\,s:!\langle(!\langle S\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},s^{\prime}:S)

and the typed value
V=\lambda x.\,x!\langle s^{\prime}\rangle.m?(z).\mathbf{0}
with

\Gamma;\emptyset;s^{\prime}:S,m:?(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\vdash V\,\triangleright\,(!\langle S\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})\!\multimap\!\diamond

Then, by Rule
{\footnotesize[\textsc{SSnd}]}
, we can derive:

(\Gamma;\emptyset;s:!\langle(!\langle S\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})\!\multimap\!\diamond\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},s^{\prime}:S)\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,m)\,s!\langle V\rangle}}(\Gamma;\emptyset;s:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\overline{m}:!\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})

Observe how the protocol along
s
is partially consumed; also, the resulting session environment is extended with

\overline{m}
, the dual endpoint of the extruded name
m
.

Notation 4.1
.

Given a value
V
of type
U
, we sometimes annotate the output action

(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\rangle

with the type of
V

as
(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,n!\langle V\!:\!U\rangle
.

\frac{\overline{s}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)\Gamma;\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash V\triangleright U}{(\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,s:?(U){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S)\mathrel{\xrightarrow{s?\langle V\rangle}}(\Gamma;\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta,\Delta^{\prime},s:S)}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{SRv}]}}\frac{\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a\triangleright\langle U\rangle\Gamma;\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash V\triangleright U}{(\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta)\mathrel{\xrightarrow{a?\langle{V}\rangle}}(\Gamma;\Lambda,\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta,\Delta^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{ShRv}]}}\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash V\triangleright U\Gamma^{\prime};\emptyset;\Delta_{j}\vdash m_{j}\triangleright U_{j}\overline{s}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)\quad\Delta^{\prime}\backslash(\cup_{j}\Delta_{j})\subseteq(\Delta,s:S)\Gamma^{\prime};\emptyset;\Delta_{j}^{\prime}\vdash\overline{m}_{j}\triangleright U_{j}^{\prime}\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda\end{array}}{(\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,s:!\langle U\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S)\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,s!\langle V\rangle}}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\Lambda\backslash\Lambda^{\prime};(\Delta,s:S,\cup_{j}\Delta_{j}^{\prime})\backslash\Delta^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{SSnd}]}}\frac{\begin{array}[]{l}\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash V\triangleright U\Gamma^{\prime};\emptyset;\Delta_{j}\vdash m_{j}\triangleright U_{j}\Gamma;\emptyset;\emptyset\vdash a\triangleright\langle U\rangle\quad\Delta^{\prime}\backslash(\cup_{j}\Delta_{j})\subseteq\Delta\Gamma^{\prime};\emptyset;\Delta_{j}^{\prime}\vdash\overline{m}_{j}\triangleright U_{j}^{\prime}\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda\end{array}}{(\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta)\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m})\,a!\langle V\rangle}}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\Lambda\backslash\Lambda^{\prime};(\Delta,\cup_{j}\Delta_{j}^{\prime})\backslash\Delta^{\prime})}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{ShSnd}]}}\frac{\Delta_{1}\longrightarrow\Delta_{2}\vee\Delta_{1}=\Delta_{2}}{(\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}(\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta_{2})}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{Tau}]}}

Figure 16:
Labeled Transition System for Typed Environments.

The typed LTS combines
the LTSs in
Figures

4
and
16
.

Definition 4.11

(Typed Labeled Transition System)
.

A
typed transition relation
is a typed relation

\Gamma;\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}\Delta_{2}\vdash P_{2}

where:

1.

P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P_{2}
and

2.

(\Gamma,\emptyset,\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}(\Gamma,\emptyset,\Delta_{2})

with
\Gamma;\emptyset;\Delta_{i}\vdash P_{i}\triangleright\diamond
(
i=1,2
).

We
write
\mathrel{\xRightarrow{}}
for the reflexive and transitive closure of
\mathrel{\xrightarrow{}}
,

\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\ell}}
for the transitions
\mathrel{\xRightarrow{}}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{}}
, and
\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\hat{\ell}}}

for
\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\ell}}
if
\ell\not=\tau
otherwise
\mathrel{\xRightarrow{}}
.

A typed transition relation requires type judgements with an empty
\Lambda
, i.e., an empty environment
for linear higher-order types.
Notice that for open process terms (i.e., with free variables),
we can always apply Rule
{\footnotesize[\textrm{\small EProm}]}
(cf.
Figure

6
) and obtain an empty
\Lambda
.
As it will be clear below (cf.
Definition

4.13
), we will be working with closed process terms, i.e., processes without free variables.

Typed Relations.

We now define
typed relations
and
contextual equivalence
(i.e., barbed congruence).
To define typed relations, we first define
confluence

over session environments
\Delta
.
Recall that

\Delta
captures session communication, which is deterministic.
The notion of confluence allows us to abstract away from alternative computation paths
that may arise due to non-interfering reductions of session names.

Definition 4.12

(Session Environment Confluence)
.

Two session environments
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}

are
confluent
, denoted
\Delta_{1}\rightleftharpoons\Delta_{2}
,
if there exists a
\Delta
such that:
i)
\Delta_{1}\longrightarrow^{\ast}\Delta
and
ii)
\Delta_{2}\longrightarrow^{\ast}\Delta

(here we write
\longrightarrow^{\ast}
for the multi-step reduction in
Definition

2.1
).

We illustrate confluence by means of an example:

Example 4.6

(Session Environment Confluence)
.

Consider the (balanced) session environments:

\displaystyle\Delta_{1}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{s_{1}:T_{1},s_{2}:?(U_{2}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\overline{s_{2}}:!\langle U_{2}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}

\displaystyle\Delta_{2}

\displaystyle=

\displaystyle\{s_{1}:T_{1},s_{2}:!\langle U_{1}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(U_{2}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\overline{s_{2}}:?(U_{1}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle U_{2}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}

Following
Definition

2.1
, we have that

\Delta_{1}\longrightarrow\{s_{1}:T_{1},s_{2}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\overline{s_{2}}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}

and
\Delta_{2}\longrightarrow\longrightarrow\{s_{1}:T_{1},s_{2}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}},\overline{s_{2}}:\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}
.
Therefore,
\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are confluent.
∎

Typed relations relate only closed processes whose
session environments are balanced and confluent:

Definition 4.13

(Typed Relation)
.

We say that a binary relation over typing judgements

\Gamma_{1};\emptyset;\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\triangleright\diamond\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash P_{2}\triangleright\diamond

is a
typed relation
whenever:

1.

P_{1}
and
P_{2}
are closed;

2.

\Delta_{1}
and
\Delta_{2}
are balanced (cf.
Definition

2.1
); and

3.

\Delta_{1}\rightleftharpoons\Delta_{2}
(cf.
Definition

4.12
).

Notation 4.2

(Typed Relations)
.

Given a typed relation
\Gamma_{1};\emptyset;\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\triangleright\diamond\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\emptyset;\Delta_{2}\vdash P_{2}\triangleright\diamond
, to reduce eye strain
we shall write:

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash P_{2}

Characteristic Bisimilarity

Characteristic bisimilarity equates typed processes by relying on
characteristic trigger processes
.
Intuitively, characteristic processes arise in characterizations of contextual equivalence to (succintly) capture the arbitrary contexts in which an exchanged name can be used by a recipient.
This notion, which we recall below, needs to be adjusted for our purposes.

\begin{array}[]{rclcrcl}[\!\!(?(C){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S)\!\!]^{u}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&u?(x).(t!\langle u\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;[\!\!(C)\!\!]^{x})&&[\!\!(S)\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&s\penalty\ \penalty\ (s\textrm{ fresh})\\
[\!\!(!\langle C\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S)\!\!]^{u}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&u!\langle[\!\!(C)\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}\rangle.t!\langle u\rangle.\mathbf{0}&&[\!\!(\langle S\rangle)\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&a\penalty\ \penalty\ (a\textrm{ fresh})\\
[\!\!(\mu\mathsf{t}.S)\!\!]^{u}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&[\!\!(S\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\})\!\!]^{u}\par&&\\
[\!\!(\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}})\!\!]^{u}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&\mathbf{0}\\
[\!\!(\langle S\rangle)\!\!]^{u}&\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}&u!\langle[\!\!(S)\!\!]_{\mathsf{c}}\rangle.t!\langle u\rangle.\mathbf{0}\\
\end{array}

Figure 17:
Characteristic processes (left) and characteristic values (right), as introduced in
Definition

4.14
.

Definition 4.14

(Characteristic trigger process
[
14
]
)
.

The
characteristic trigger process
for type
C
is

\displaystyle t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}t?(x).(\nu\,s)(s?(y).[\!\!(C)\!\!]^{y}\;|\;\overline{s}!\langle v\rangle.\mathbf{0})

where
[\!\!(C)\!\!]^{y}
is the
characteristic process

for
C
on name
y
(cf.
Figure

17
).

We may now state the definition of characteristic bisimilarity that applies in our (first-order) setting:

Definition 4.15

(Characteristic Bisimilarity)
.

A typed relation
\Re
is a
characteristic bisimulation
if
for all
\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}
,

1)

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle V_{1}:U_{1}\rangle}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist

Q_{2}
,
V_{2}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,n!\langle V_{2}:U_{2}\rangle}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and, for a fresh
t
,

\Gamma_{1};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{1}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\;|\;t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}V_{1}{\,:\,}U_{1})}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{2}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\;|\;t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}V_{2}{\,:\,}U_{2})}

2)

For all
\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}
such that

\ell
is not an output, there exist
Q_{2}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\hat{\ell}}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}
; and

3)

The symmetric cases of 1 and 2.

The largest such bisimulation is called
characteristic bisimilarity
, denoted by
\approx^{\mathtt{C}}
.

4.7.2
MST-bisimilarity and Main Result

We introduce MST-bisimilarity (denoted
\approx^{\mathtt{M}}
), and discuss key differences with respect to characteristic bisimilarity.
One of the differences is that we let an action along a name
n
to be mimicked by an action on a possibly indexed name
n_{i}
, for some
i
.

Definition 4.16

(Indexed name)
.

Given a name
n
, we write
\breve{n}
to either denote

n
or any indexed name
n_{i}
, with
i>0
.

Suppose we wish to relate
P
and
Q
using
\approx^{\mathtt{M}}
, and that
P
performs an
output action involving name
v
. In our setting,
Q
should send a
tuple

of names: the decomposition of
v
. Another difference is that output
objects should be related by the relation
\diamond
:

Definition 4.17

(Relating names)
.

We define the relation on names
\diamond
as follows:

\frac{}{\epsilon\diamond\epsilon}\qquad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta\vdash n_{i}\triangleright C}{n_{i}\diamond(n_{i},\ldots,n_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|-1})}\qquad\frac{\tilde{n}\diamond\tilde{m}_{1}\ \quad n_{i}\diamond\tilde{m}_{2}}{\tilde{n},n_{i}\diamond\tilde{m}_{1},\tilde{m}_{2}}

where
\epsilon
denotes the empty list.

Our variants of characteristic and trigger processes are defined as follows:

Definition 4.18

(Minimal characteristic processes)
.

Given a type
C
, name
u
, and index
i
, we define the
minimal
characteristic processes
\langle C\rangle^{u}_{i}
and
\langle C\rangle_{\texttt{c}}
as follows:

\displaystyle\langle?(C){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\rangle^{u}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u_{i}?(x).(t_{1}!\langle u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|}\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;\langle C\rangle^{x}_{i})

\displaystyle\langle!\langle C\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S\rangle^{u}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u_{i}!\langle\langle C\rangle_{\texttt{c}}\rangle.t_{1}!\langle u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\langle\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\rangle^{u}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\langle\langle C\rangle\rangle^{u}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}u_{1}!\langle\langle C\rangle_{\texttt{c}}\rangle.t_{1}!\langle u_{1}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle\langle\mu\mathsf{t}.S\rangle^{u}_{i}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}\langle S\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\mathsf{t}$}\}\rangle^{u}_{i}

\displaystyle\langle S\rangle_{\texttt{c}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}\widetilde{s}\penalty\ \penalty\ (|\widetilde{s}|=|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|,\widetilde{s}\textrm{ fresh})

\displaystyle\langle\langle C\rangle\rangle_{\texttt{c}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}a_{1}\penalty\ \penalty\ (a_{1}\textrm{ fresh})

where
t_{1}
is a fresh (indexed) name.

Given this definition, we may now revise
Definition

4.14
.

Definition 4.19

(Minimal characteristic trigger process)
.

Given a type
C
, the trigger process is

\displaystyle t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v_{i}{\,:\,}C

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathtt{def}\ }}{{=}}t_{1}?(x).(\nu\,s_{1})(s_{1}?(\widetilde{y}).\langle C\rangle^{y}_{i}\;|\;\overline{s_{1}}!\langle\tilde{v}\rangle.\mathbf{0})

where

v_{i}\diamond\widetilde{v}
,
y_{i}\diamond\widetilde{y}
, and

\langle C\rangle^{y}_{i}
is a
minimal characteristic process for type
C
on name
y

(see
Definition

4.18
).

We are now ready to define MST-bisimilarity.
In the following, we shall adopt
4.2
(2) for typed relations in order to explicitly account for the effect of the decompositions in the types/assignments recorded in
\Gamma
.

Definition 4.20

(MST-Bisimilarity)
.

A typed relation
\Re
is an
MST bisimulation
if
for all

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}
,

1.

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle v:C_{1}\rangle}}\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist

Q_{2}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\sigma_{v}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,\breve{n}!\langle\tilde{v}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\rangle}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where

v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and, for a fresh
t
,

\Gamma_{1};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{1}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\;|\;t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta^{\prime\prime}_{2}\vdash{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\;|\;t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma{\,:\,}C_{1})}

2.

Whenever
\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?(v)}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist
Q_{2}
,
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\sigma_{v}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\breve{n}?(\tilde{v})}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where

v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}
,

3.

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}
, with

\ell
not an output or input, then there exist

Q_{2}
and
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\hat{\ell}}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}\ \Re\ \Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and

\textsf{sub}(\ell)=n
implies

\textsf{sub}(\hat{\ell})=\breve{n}
.

4.

The symmetric cases of 1, 2, and 3.

The largest such bisimulation is called
MST bisimilarity
(
\approx^{\mathtt{M}}
).

We can now state our dynamic correctness result:

Theorem 4.2

(Operational Correspondence)
.

Let
P
be a process such that

\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P
. We have

\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P\ \approx^{\mathtt{M}}\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Gamma{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})};{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\Delta{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\vdash{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

The proof of this theorem is by coinduction: we exhibit a binary relation
\mathcal{S}
such that
(P,{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})\in\mathcal{S}

and is an MST bisimulation.
The proof
that
\mathcal{S}
is an MST bisimulation is given by
Lemma

4.7
and

Lemma

4.10
in the next sub-section.

4.8
Proof of
Theorem

4.2

4.8.1
Preliminaries

In this section we define the relation
\mathcal{S}
used in the proof of
Theorem

4.2
.
We require some auxiliary notions.

First, we define a relation
\diamond
on processes, which corresponds to the extension of the relation on indexed names given by
Definition

4.17
:

Definition 4.21

(Relation
\diamond
on processes)
.

Given the relation
\diamond
on names (
Definition

4.17
), we define the relation
\diamond
on processes as follows:

\frac{P\sigma\diamond P^{\prime}v\sigma\diamond\widetilde{v}\sigma=\mathsf{next}(n_{i})}{n_{i}!\langle v\rangle.P\diamond n_{i}!\langle\widetilde{v}\rangle.P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPSnd}]}}\qquad\frac{}{\mathbf{0}\diamond\mathbf{0}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPInact}]}}\\
\frac{P\sigma\diamond P^{\prime}v\sigma\diamond\widetilde{v}\sigma=\mathsf{next}(n_{i})}{n_{i}?(y).P\diamond n_{i}?(\widetilde{y}).P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPRcv}]}}\quad\frac{P\diamond P^{\prime}\tilde{m}_{1}\diamond\tilde{m}_{2}}{(\nu\,\tilde{m}_{1})\,P\diamond(\nu\,\tilde{m}_{2})\,P^{\prime}}\quad\textsc{{\footnotesize[{IPNews}]}}

We have the following properties for
\diamond
:

Lemma 4.4
.

We have:

(t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$t_{1},v_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$t,v$}\},\ t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v_{i}{\,:\,}C)\in\diamond

for
i>0
.

Proof.

Directly by
Definitions

4.14
,
4.19
and
4.21
.
∎

Lemma 4.5
.

Relation
\diamond
is an MST bisimulation.

Proof.

Straightforward by transition induction.
∎

As we have seen, the output clause of MST bisimilarity “appends” trigger processes in parallel to the processes under comparison.
The following definitions introduce notations which are useful for distinguishing the triggers included in a process.

Definition 4.22

(Trigger Collections)
.

•

We let
H,H^{\prime}
to range over
trigger collections
: processes of the form

P_{1}\;|\;\cdots\;|\;P_{n}
(with
n\geq 1
), where each
P_{i}
is a trigger
process or a process that originates from a trigger process.

•

We write

P\parallel Q
to stand for
P\;|\;Q

where either
P
or
Q
is a trigger collection.

Example 4.7
.

Let
H_{1}=t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C\;|\;[\!\!(C)\!\!]^{u}\;|\;t^{\prime}!\langle n\rangle.\mathbf{0}

where
v,t,t^{\prime},u,n
are channel names,

C
a channel type.
We could see that
[\!\!(C)\!\!]^{u}
and
t^{\prime}!\langle n\rangle.\mathbf{0}
originate from a trigger process. Thus,
H_{1}
is a trigger collection.

Definition 4.23

(Propagators of
P
)
.

We define
\mathsf{fpn}(P)
to denote the set of free propagator names in
P
.

The following definition is useful when constructing an MST bisimulation for
recursive processes
, i.e., processes
P
such that
\mathsf{frv}(P)
holds (cf.
Definition

4.5
).
The
depth
of a recursive variable denotes the number of prefixes
preceding the occurrence of a recursive variable
X
or a subprocess of the form
\mu X.Q
; in turn, this depth will be related to the current trio mimicking a given process.

Definition 4.24

(Depth of recursive variable)
.

Let
P
be a recursive process.
The functions
\delta(P)
and
\widehat{\delta}(P)
are mutually defined as follows:

\displaystyle\delta(P)

\displaystyle=\begin{cases}\widehat{\delta}(P)&\text{ if }\mathsf{frv}(P)\\
0&\text{ otherwise }\\
\end{cases}\qquad\qquad\widehat{\delta}(P)=\begin{cases}0&\text{ if }P=\mu X.Q\text{ or }P=X\\
\delta(Q)+1&\text{ if }P=\alpha.Q\\
\delta(Q)+\delta(R)&\text{ if }P=Q\;|\;R\\
\delta(Q)&\text{ if }P=(\nu\,s)\,Q\end{cases}

Definition 4.25
.

The predicate
\mathscr{D}_{X}(P,Q,d)
holds whenever there exist
Q,Q^{\prime}
such that
(i)
P\equiv Q^{\prime}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\mu X.Q$}\!/\mbox{\small$X$}\}

and
(ii)
\delta(Q^{\prime})=d
.

).

Definition 4.26

(Indexed names substitutions)
.

Let

\widetilde{u}=(a,b,s,\overline{s},\overline{s}^{\prime},s^{\prime},r,r^{\prime},\ldots)

be a finite tuple of names.
We write
\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})
to denote

\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u})=\left\{\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$a_{1},b_{1},s_{i},\overline{s}_{i},s^{\prime}_{j},\overline{s}^{\prime}_{j},r_{1},r^{\prime}_{1},\ldots$}\!/\mbox{\small$a,b,s,s^{\prime},r,r^{\prime},\ldots$}\}:i,j,\ldots>0\right\}

4.8.2
The relation
\mathcal{S}
: Ingredients and Properties

Having all auxiliary notions in place, we are ready to define

\mathcal{S}
:

Definition 4.27

(Relation
\mathcal{S}
)
.

Let
P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
be a well-typed process, with
\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{C}
.
We define the relation
\mathcal{S}
as follows:

\displaystyle\mathcal{S}

\displaystyle=\{(P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\},R)\,:\,R\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{w}}_{\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\sigma\in\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{u}\cup\widetilde{x}\cup\mathsf{fn}(P)),\penalty\ \widetilde{w}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{u}\sigma:\widetilde{C}),\penalty\ \widetilde{y}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}\sigma:\widetilde{C})\}

where the set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
as in
Table

7
.

The definition of
\mathcal{S}
follows Parrow’s proof of dynamic correctness for the trios in the untyped
\pi
-calculus.
Intuitively, processes in the set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\widetilde{w}}_{\widetilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
represent processes that are “correlated” to
P
, up to synchronizations induced by propagators.
In our case, the presence of trigger processes and recursive processes induces significant differences with respect to Parrow’s definition.
Given a process
P
, we have two mutually defined sets:
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathscr{J}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
, which are both given in
Table

7
. The idea is that
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
deals with trigger processes and top-level activation of propagators, whereas
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathscr{J}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
collects processes without triggers that are involved in the overall activation of propagators.
Handling recursive processes requires dedicated treatment; this is formalized by the auxiliary sets
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathcal{C}}}^{\,\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
(defined in
Table

8
) and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathscr{J}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{x},\rho}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}^{d}_{g}
(defined in

Tables

9
and
10
).

Properties.

We prove a series of lemmas that establish a form of
operational correspondence
, divided in completeness and soundness properties.
We first need the following result. Following Parrow
[
18
]
, we refer to prefixes
that do not correspond to prefixes of the original process (i.e. prefixes on propagators
c_{i}
),
as
non-essential prefixes
.
The relation
\mathcal{S}
is closed under reductions that involve non-essential prefixes.

Lemma 4.6
.

Given an indexed process
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
,
the set
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
is closed under
\tau
transitions on
non-essential prefixes. That is,
if
R_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

and
R_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}R_{2}
is inferred from the actions on non-essential prefixes,
then
R_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
.

Proof (Sketch).

By the induction on the structure of
P_{1}
and
the inspection of definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathscr{J}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

in
Table

7
and
Table

8
.
∎

Operational Completeness.

We first consider transitions using the untyped LTS; in
Lemma

4.8
we will
consider transitions with the typed LTS.

Lemma 4.7
.

Assume
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
is a
process and
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathcal{S}Q_{1}
.

1.

Whenever

{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle v:C_{1}\rangle}}}{P_{2}}
,
such that
\overline{n}\not\in P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
,
then there exist

Q_{2}
and
\sigma_{v}
such that

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xRightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,\breve{n}!\langle\widetilde{v}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\rangle}}}{Q_{2}}

where
v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and, for a fresh
t
,

{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})}\ \mathcal{S}\ {(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})}

2.

Whenever
{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?(v)}}}{P_{2}}
,
such that
\overline{n}\not\in P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
,
then there exist
Q_{2}
and
\sigma_{v}
such that

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\breve{n}?(\tilde{v})}}}{Q_{2}}

where
v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and

{P_{2}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}
,

3.

Whenever
{P_{1}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}{P_{2}}

then there exists
Q_{2}
such that

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\tau}}}{Q_{2}}

and

{P_{2}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}
.

Proof.

By transition induction. See
Section

A.6
for details.
∎

The following statement builds upon the previous one to the case of
typed LTS (
Definition

4.11
):

Lemma 4.8
.

Assume
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
is a
process and
