.

1.

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle v:C_{1}\rangle}}\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist

Q_{2}
,
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
, and
\sigma_{v}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,\breve{n}!\langle\tilde{v}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\rangle}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where

v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and, for a fresh
t
,

{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})}\ \mathcal{S}\ {(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})}

2.

Whenever
\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?(v)}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist
Q_{2}
,
\Delta_{2}^{\prime}
, and
\sigma_{v}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\breve{n}?(\tilde{v})}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

where

v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and

P_{2}\ \mathcal{S}\ Q_{2}
,

3.

Whenever

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

then there exist

Q_{2}
and
\Delta^{\prime}_{2}
such that

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{{\tau}}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

and

P_{2}\ \mathcal{S}\ Q_{2}
.

Proof.

The proof uses results of
Lemma

4.7
.
We consider the first case, as the other two are similar.
By the definition of the typed LTS (
Definition

4.11
) we have:

\displaystyle\Gamma_{1};\Lambda_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\triangleright\diamond

(3)

\displaystyle(\Gamma_{1};\emptyset;\Delta_{1})\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle v:C_{1}\rangle}}(\Gamma_{1};\emptyset;\Delta_{2})

(4)

By (
4
) we further have

\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\Lambda^{\prime};\Delta^{\prime}\vdash V\triangleright U

\Delta^{\prime}\backslash(\cup_{j}\Delta_{j})\subseteq(\Delta,n:S)

\Gamma^{\prime};\emptyset;\Delta_{j}\vdash m_{j}\triangleright U_{j}

\Gamma^{\prime};\emptyset;\Delta_{j}^{\prime}\vdash\overline{m}_{j}\triangleright U_{j}^{\prime}

\overline{n}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)

\Lambda^{\prime}\subseteq\Lambda

[
SSnd
]

(\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,n:!\langle C_{1}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}S)\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle v:C_{1}\rangle}}(\Gamma,\Gamma^{\prime};\Lambda\backslash\Lambda^{\prime};(\Delta,n:S,\cup_{j}\Delta_{j}^{\prime})\backslash\Delta^{\prime})

By
(
3
)
and the condition
\overline{n}\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Delta)

we have
\overline{n}\not\in\mathsf{fn}(P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\})
.
Therefore, we can apply Item 1 of
Lemma

4.7
.

∎

Operational Soundness.

For the proof of operational soundness we follow the same strategy as before: we first establish a lemma for the untyped LTS, then we extend it to the case of the typed LTS.

Lemma 4.9
.

Assume
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
is a
process and
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\ \mathcal{S}\ Q_{1}
.

1.

Whenever

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,n_{i}!\langle\widetilde{v}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\rangle}}}{Q_{2}}

, such that
\overline{n}_{i}\not\in\mathsf{fn}(Q_{1})
,
then there exist

P_{2}
and
\sigma_{v}
such that

{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle v:C_{1}\rangle}}}{P_{2}}

where
v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

where
v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and, for a fresh
t
,

{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})}\ \mathcal{S}\ {(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})}

2.

Whenever

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\breve{n}?(\tilde{v})}}}{Q_{2}}

then there exist
P_{2}
and
\sigma_{v}
such that

{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n?(v)}}}{P_{2}}

where
v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and

{P_{2}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}
,

3.

Whenever

{Q_{1}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}{Q_{2}}

either (i)
{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}

or
(ii)
there exists
P_{2}
such that

{P_{1}}{\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}}{P_{2}}

and

{P_{2}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}
.

Proof (Sketch).

Following Parrow’s approach, we refer to prefixes
corresponding to prefixes of the original process
as
essential prefixes
.
We remark that a prefix in

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
is non-essential if and only if is a prefix on a propagator name.
First, we discuss the case when a transition is inferred without any
actions from essential prefixes. In this case
we know that an action can only involve propagator prefixes and
by inspection of definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
that

\ell=\tau
.
This concerns the sub-case (i) of Part 3
and it directly follows by
Lemma

4.6
.

Now, assume
Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}Q_{2}
when
\ell
involves essential prefixes.
This concerns Part 1, Part 2, and
sub-case (ii) of Part 3. This case is mainly the inverse of the proof
of
Lemma

4.7
. As Parrow, here we note that
an essential prefix is unguarded in
Q_{1}
if and only if it is
unguarded in
P_{1}
. That is, by inspection of the definition, we see that
function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u}}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

does not unguard essential prefixes of
P_{1}
that its members mimic
(the propagators serve as guards).
∎

Lemma 4.10
.

Assume
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
is a
process and
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\ \mathcal{S}\ Q_{1}
.

1.

Whenever

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})\,\breve{n}!\langle\widetilde{v}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}C_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\rangle}}\Delta^{\prime}_{2}\vdash Q_{2}

then there exist

P_{2}
,
\sigma_{v}
, and
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
such that

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})\,n!\langle v:C_{1}\rangle}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

and, for a fresh
t
,

{(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{1}})(P_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})}\ \mathcal{S}\ {(\nu\,\widetilde{m_{2}})(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})}

2.

Whenever

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\breve{n}?(\tilde{v})}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

then there exist

P_{2}
,
\sigma_{v}
, and
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
such that

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{{n}?(v)}}\Delta^{\prime}_{1}\vdash P_{2}

where
v\sigma_{v}\diamond\widetilde{v}

and

{P_{2}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}
,

3.

Whenever

\Gamma_{2};\Delta_{2}\vdash Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}\Delta_{2}^{\prime}\vdash Q_{2}

either (i)

{P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}

or
(ii)
there exist
P_{2}
and
\Delta_{1}^{\prime}
such that

\Gamma_{1};\Delta_{1}\vdash P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{\tau}}\Delta_{1}^{\prime}\vdash P_{2}

and

{P_{2}}{\ \mathcal{S}\ }{Q_{2}}
.

Proof (Sketch).

Analogous to the proof of
Lemma

4.8
, using

Lemma

4.9
.

∎

We close this section by stating again
Theorem

4.2
and giving its proof.

See
4.2

Proof.

By
Lemmas

4.9
and
4.10
we know
\mathcal{S}
is an MST bisimulation.
So, we need to show
(P,{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})\in\mathcal{S}
.
Let
P_{1}
be such that
P_{1}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{r}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}=P

where
\widetilde{r}=\mathsf{rn}(P)
. Further, let
\sigma=\bigcup_{v\in\tilde{r}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$v_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$v$}\}
,
so we have
\sigma\in\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{r})
. Then, let
\widetilde{r_{*}}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{r}:\widetilde{S})

and
\widetilde{x_{*}}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}:\widetilde{S})
where
\widetilde{r}:\widetilde{S}
.
Therefore, by
Definition

4.8
and
Table

7

we have
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{r_{*}}}_{\tilde{x_{*}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
.
Finally, by
Definition

4.27
we have
(P,{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}P}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})\in\mathcal{S}
.
∎

5
Extension with Labeled Choices

In this section, we briefly discuss the extension of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
with
labeled choices
.
In session-based concurrency, labeled choices are implemented via branching and selection constructs, denoted
u_{i}\triangleright\{l_{j}:P_{j}\}_{j\in I}
(for some finite set
I
) and
u_{i}\triangleleft l_{j}.Q
, respectively. Intuitively, a branching construct specifies the offer of a finite number of alternative behaviors (
P_{1},P_{2},\ldots
), whereas a selection construct signals the choice of one of them.
Put differently, branching and selection implement deterministic choices via the input and output of labels; the reduction rule is as follows:

n\triangleleft l_{j}.Q\;|\;\overline{n}\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\longrightarrow Q\;|\;P_{j}\quad(j\in I)

Branching and selection come with dedicated session types, denoted

\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
and
\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, respectively, for some finite index set
I
.
The corresponding typing rules are:

\frac{\forall i\in I\quad\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:S_{i}\vdash P_{i}\triangleright\diamond}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}\vdash u\triangleright\{l_{i}:P_{i}\}_{i\in I}\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Bra)}\qquad\frac{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:S_{j}\vdash P\triangleright\diamond\quad j\in I}{\Gamma;\Lambda;\Delta,u:\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}\vdash u\triangleleft l_{j}.P\triangleright\diamond}\quad\textsc{(Sel)}

Our decomposition strategy can be extended to account for labeled choice (and their types).
This entails the extension of the type decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
(
Definition

4.1
) and of the breakdown function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
(
Table

5
); this latter extension will be denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
.

For simplicity, we focus on
finite
processes, without recursion / recursive session types. One of the challenges involved in extending our approach to account for both labeled choices and recursion concerns
non-uniform
termination: in a branching type
\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
not only each
S_{i}
can be different, but some of them may denote recursive (non terminating) behaviors while the rest may not. Handling this potential non-uniformity requires care.
We base our presentation on the corresponding breakdown for the case of the higher-order calculus
\mathsf{HO}
, as presented in
[
2
]
.

To uniformly handle the potential differences in a branching type
\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}
, we break down branching and selection types as follows:

Definition 5.1

(Decomposing Types: Labeled Choices)
.

We extend the type decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
(
Definition

4.1
) as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\&\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

\displaystyle=\&\{l_{i}:\,?({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}\oplus\{l_{i}:S_{i}\}_{i\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

\displaystyle=\oplus\{l_{i}:\,!\langle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}\}_{i\in I}

This decomposition follows the intuition that branching and selection correspond to the input and output of labels, respectively.

Definition 5.2

(Decomposing Processes: Labeled Choice)
.

Given a
k\geq 0
and a tuple of names
\tilde{x}
, the decomposition function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
is defined as

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}u_{i}\triangleright\{l_{j}:P_{j}\}_{j\in I}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?(\tilde{x}).u_{i}\triangleright\{l_{j}:(\nu\,\tilde{c}_{j})\,u_{i}?(\tilde{y}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\tilde{x}\tilde{y}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k+1}_{\tilde{x}\tilde{y}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P_{j}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{i}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\}_{j\in I}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}u_{i}\triangleleft l_{j}.Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{k}?(\tilde{x}).u_{i}\triangleleft l_{j}.(\nu\,\tilde{u}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S_{j}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})})\,u_{i}!\langle{\tilde{\overline{u}}}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\tilde{x}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k+1}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$s_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{i}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

where
\widetilde{u}=(u_{i+1},\ldots,u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S_{j}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|})

and
\widetilde{\overline{u}}=(\overline{u_{i+1}},\ldots,\overline{u_{i+|{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S_{j}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}|}})
.

For the remaining constructs,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
corresponds
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
(
Table

5
).

Observe how, in the case of branching, once a particular branch
l_{i}
has been selected, we receive the names on which to provide sessions from the branch
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
; the corresponding selection process communicates these names.
To account for the different session types of the branches
S_{i}
, the names involved in the decomposition are bound (i.e., hidden).

We illustrate the workings of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{k}_{\tilde{x}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
by means of
the following example, adapted from
[
2
]
:

Example 5.1
.

Consider a mathematical server
Q
that offers clients two operations: addition and negation of integers.
The server uses name
u
to implement the following session type:

\displaystyle S=\&\{\textsf{add}:\underbrace{?(\textsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}?(\textsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}_{S_{\textsf{add}}}\penalty\ ,\ \textsf{neg}:\underbrace{?(\textsf{int}){\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25};}\mathtt{{\color[rgb]{0.25,0.25,0.25}end}}}_{S_{\textsf{neg}}}\}

This way, the
add
branch receives two integers and sends
over their sum; the
neg
branch has a single input of an integer followed by an
output of its negation.

Let us consider a possible implementation for the server
Q
and for a client
R
that selects the first branch to add integers 16 and 26:

\displaystyle Q

\displaystyle\triangleq u\triangleright\{\textsf{add}:\underbrace{u?(a).u?(b).u!\langle a+b\rangle}_{Q_{\textsf{add}}},\ \textsf{neg}:\underbrace{u?(a).u!\langle-a\rangle}_{Q_{\textsf{neg}}}\}

\displaystyle R

\displaystyle\triangleq\overline{u}\triangleleft\textsf{add}.\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}?(r)

The composed process
P\triangleq(\nu\,u)\,(Q\;|\;R)
can reduce as follows:

\displaystyle P

\displaystyle\penalty\ \longrightarrow\penalty\ (\nu\,u)\,(u?(a).u?(b).u!\langle a+b\rangle\;|\;\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}?(r))

\displaystyle\penalty\ \longrightarrow^{2}\penalty\ (\nu\,u)\,(u!\langle\mathbf{16+26}\rangle\;|\;\overline{u}?(r))=P^{\prime}

First, following
Definition

5.1
, the decomposition of
S
is the minimal session type
M
, defined as follows:

\displaystyle M={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{H}}^{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}*}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}(}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=\&\{

\displaystyle\textsf{add}:?({\big(?(\textsf{int}),?(\textsf{int}),!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle\big)}),

\displaystyle\textsf{neg}:?({\big(?(\textsf{int}),!\langle\textsf{int}\rangle\big)})\}

Let us now discuss the decomposition of
P
.
Using
Definition

5.2
we have:

\displaystyle D=(\nu\,c_{1},\ldots,c_{7})\,(c_{1}?().\overline{c_{2}}!\langle\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{2}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{3}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)})

(5)

where
\sigma_{2}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u_{1}\overline{u_{1}}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u\overline{u}$}\}
.
The breakdown of process
Q
is as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{2}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}=c_{2}?().u_{1}\triangleright\{

\displaystyle\textsf{add}:(\nu\,\tilde{c}_{j})\,u_{1}?(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}).\overline{c_{1}}!\langle y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{add}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)},

\displaystyle\textsf{neg}:(\nu\,\tilde{c}_{j})\,u_{1}?(\tilde{y}).\overline{c_{1}}!\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{neg}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\}

where

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{add}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{1}?(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3})\;|\;c_{1}?(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}).y_{1}?(a).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle y_{2},y_{3},a\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{2}?(y_{2},y_{3},a).y_{2}?(b).\overline{c_{3}}!\langle y_{3},a,b\rangle\;|\;c_{3}?(y_{3},a,b).y_{3}!\langle a+b\rangle.c_{4}?()\;|\;c_{4}?()

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{neg}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{1}?(y_{1},y_{2})\;|\;c_{1}?(y_{1},y_{2}).y_{1}?(a).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle y_{2},a\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{2}?(y_{2},a).y_{2}!\langle-a\rangle.\overline{c_{3}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{3}?()

In process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{add}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
, name
u_{1}
implements
M
.
Following the common trio structure, the first prefix awaits activation on

c_{2}
. The next prefix mimics the
branching action of
Q
on
u_{1}
.
Then, each branch consists of the input of
the breakdown of the continuation of channel
u
, that is

y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}
. This input does not have a counterpart in
Q
;
it is meant to synchronize with process
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{3}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
,
the breakdown of the corresponding selection process.

In the bodies of the abstractions we break down
Q_{\textsf{add}}
and
Q_{\textsf{neg}}
, but not before adjusting the names on which the broken down processes provide the sessions.
For this, we substitute
u
with
y_{1}
in both processes, ensuring that the broken down names are bound by the input.
By binding decomposed names in the input we account for different
session types of the original name in branches, while preserving typability:
this way the decomposition of different branches can use (i) the same names
but typed with different minimal types and (ii) a different number of names, as it is the
case in this example.

The decomposition of the client process
R
, which implements the selection,
is as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{3}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle=c_{3}?(\epsilon).\overline{u}_{1}\triangleleft\mathsf{add}.(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,\overline{u}_{1}!\langle u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{4}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}?(r){\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

where:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{4}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}?(r){\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}=c_{4}?().\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()

After receiving the context on
c_{3}
(empty in this case), the selection action on
u_{1}
is mimicked; then, the breakdown of
channel continuation,
u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}
, that are locally bound,
are sent along name
u_{1}
.
The intention is to use these names to connect
the selected branch and the continuation of a selection process: the subprocess encapsulated in the branch will use

(u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})
, while the dual names
(\overline{u}_{2},\overline{u}_{3},\overline{u}_{4})
are present in the breakdown of the continuation.

Let us briefly examine the reductions of the decomposed process
D
in (
5
).
First,
c_{1}
,
c_{2}
, and
c_{3}
will synchronize.
We have
D\longrightarrow^{4}D_{1}
, where

\displaystyle D_{1}=(\nu\,c_{4}\ldots c_{7})\,

\displaystyle(\nu\,u_{1})\,\big(u_{1}\triangleright\{\textsf{add}:(\nu\,\tilde{c}_{j})\,u_{1}?(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}).\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\tilde{y}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{add}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)},

\displaystyle\qquad\qquad\qquad\textsf{neg}:(\nu\,\tilde{c}_{j})\,u_{1}?(\tilde{y}).\overline{c_{1}}!\langle y_{1},y_{2}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{neg}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\}

\displaystyle\qquad\quad\;|\;\overline{u}_{1}\triangleleft\mathsf{add}.(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,\overline{u}_{1}!\langle u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{4}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}?(r){\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\big)

Now, the processes chooses the label
add

on
u_{1}
. The process
D_{1}
will reduce further as

D_{1}\longrightarrow D_{2}\longrightarrow^{2}D_{3}
, where:

\displaystyle D_{2}=\penalty\

\displaystyle(\nu\,\tilde{c}_{j})\,u_{1}?(y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}).\overline{c_{1}}!\langle\tilde{y}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{1}_{y_{1},y_{2},y_{3}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}Q_{\mathsf{add}}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$u_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad(\nu\,u_{2},u_{3},u_{4})\,\overline{u}_{1}!\langle u_{2},u_{3},u_{4}\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}^{\boxplus}}^{4}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{u}_{2}?(r){\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

\displaystyle D_{3}=\penalty\

\displaystyle\overline{u}_{2}?(a).\overline{c_{2}}!\langle u_{3},u_{4},a\rangle\;|\;c_{2}?(y_{2},y_{3},a).y_{2}?(b).\overline{c_{3}}!\langle y_{3},a,b\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{3}?(y_{3},a,b).y_{3}!\langle a+b\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{4}?().\overline{u}_{2}!\langle\mathbf{16}\rangle.\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()

Now, process
D_{3}
can mimic the original
transmission of the integer
16
on channel
u_{2}
as follows:

\displaystyle D_{3}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow\overline{c_{2}}!\langle\overline{u}_{3},\overline{u}_{4},\mathbf{16}\rangle\;|\;c_{2}?(y_{2},y_{3},a).y_{2}?(b).\overline{c_{3}}!\langle y_{3},a,b\rangle\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad c_{3}?(y_{3},a,b).y_{3}!\langle a+b\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{4}?()\;|\;

\displaystyle\qquad\overline{c_{5}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{5}?().\overline{u}_{3}!\langle\mathbf{26}\rangle.\overline{c_{6}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{6}?().\overline{u}_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()=D_{4}

Finally,
D_{4}
reduces to
D_{5}
as follows:

\displaystyle D_{4}

\displaystyle\longrightarrow^{5}\overline{u}_{4}!\langle a+b\rangle.\overline{c_{4}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{4}?()\;|\;u_{4}?(r).\overline{c_{7}}!\langle\rangle\;|\;c_{7}?()=D_{5}

This way, the steps from
D
to
D_{5}
attest to the fact that our extended decomposition correctly simulates the steps from
P
to
P^{\prime}
.

6
Related Work

Closely related work has been already discussed throughout the paper. In this section, we comment on other related literature.

A source of inspiration for our developments is the trios decomposition by Parrow
[
18
]
, which he studied for an untyped
\pi
-calculus with replication; in contrast,
\pi
processes are typed and feature recursion.
We stress that our goal is to clarify the role of sequentiality in session types by using processes with MSTs, which lack sequentiality.
While Parrow’s approach elegantly induces processes typable with MSTs (and suggests a clean approach to establish dynamic correctness), defining trios decompositions for
\pi
is just one path towards our goal.

The present work differs significantly with respect to our previous work
[
3
,
2
]
, which used
\mathsf{HO}
as
source language. Session communication in
\mathsf{HO}
is based on abstraction-passing, whereas here we focus on the name-passing calculus
\pi
.
This difference has several ramifications.
While in
\mathsf{HO}
propagators carry abstractions,
in our case propagators
are binding and carry names. Also, names must be decomposed and propagating them requires care.
Further novelties appear when decomposing processes with recursion, which require a dedicated collection of
recursive trios
; in contrast, an explicit construct for recursion is not present in
\mathsf{HO}
.
The proof of dynamic correctness for
\pi
shares some similarities with the same proof for
\mathsf{HO}
, given in
[
2
]
; however, the technical details of moving from higher-order concurrency to first-order concurrency are substantial—from the behavioral equivalences used to the construction of the required bisimilarities (cf.
Section

4.8
).

Our work aims to understand session types in terms of themselves, by considering to the sub-class of session types without sequentiality, as defined by MSTs.
Prior works have related session types with
different
type systems—see, e.g.,
[
11
,
5
,
6
,
7
,
8
]
.
Kobayashi
[
11
]
was the first to define a formal relationship between session types and
usage types
, expressed as typed encodings of processes; this relationship was thoroughly studied by Dardha et al.
[
5
,
6
,
4
]
(see below).
Demangeon and Honda
[
7
]
connect a linearly-typed
\pi
-calculus with subtyping and a session-typed calculus via a full abstraction result.
Both works rely on convenient constructs in the target language (e.g., case constructors and variant types) to achieve correct encodability. The work of Gay et al.
[
8
]
addresses a similar problem but by adopting a
\pi
-calculus without such additional features: they consider the correct encodability of session types into a generic type system for a simple
\pi
-calculus. Their work demonstrates that the translation of session types for branching and selection in the presence of subtyping is a challenging endeavor.

The work by Dardha et al.
[
5
,
6
,
4
]
develops further the translation first suggested by Kobayashi
[
11
]
.
They compile a session
\pi
-calculus down into a
\pi
-calculus with the linear type system of
[
12
]
extended with variant types.
They represent sequentiality using a continuation-passing style (CPS): a session type is interpreted as a linear type carrying a pair, consisting of
the original payload type and a new linear channel type, to be used for ensuing interactions.
The differences between this CPS approach and our work are also technical: the approach in
[
5
]
thus involves translations connecting
two
different
\pi
-calculi and
two
different type systems. In contrast, our approach based on MSTs justifies sequentiality using a single typed process framework.
Another difference concerns process recursion and recursive session types: while the works
[
5
,
6
]
consider only finite processes (no recursion nor recursive types), the work
[
4
]
considers recursive session types as a way of supporting replicated processes (a specific class of unrestricted behaviors). In contrast, the decompositions we have considered here support full process recursion.

Despite these concrete differences, it is interesting to compare our approach and the CPS approach.
To substantiate our comparisons, we introduce some selected notions for the linearly-typed
\pi
-calculus considered by Dardha et al. (the reader is referred to
[
6
]
for a thorough description and technical results).

Definition 6.1

(Linearly-Typed
\pi
-calculus Processes
[
6
]
)
.

The syntax of processes
P,Q,\ldots
, values
v,v^{\prime},\ldots
, and of linear types
\tau,\tau^{\prime},\ldots
is as follows:

\displaystyle\text{Processes\penalty\ \penalty\ }P,Q,

\displaystyle:=x!\langle\tilde{v}\rangle.P\;\;{|}\;\;x?(\tilde{y}).P\;\;{|}\;\;(\nu\,v)\,P\;\;{|}\;\;\mathbf{0}\;\;{|}\;\;P\;|\;Q\;\;{|}\;\;\textbf{case}\ v\ \textbf{of}\ \{l_{i}\_(x_{i})\triangleright P_{i}\}_{i\in I}

\displaystyle\text{Values\penalty\ \penalty\ }v,v^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=x\;\;{|}\;\;\star\;\;{|}\;\;l\_v

\displaystyle\text{Types\penalty\ \penalty\ }\tau,\tau^{\prime}

\displaystyle:=l_{\mathtt{o}}[\widetilde{\tau}]\;\;{|}\;\;l_{\mathtt{i}}[\widetilde{\tau}]\;\;{|}\;\;l_{\#}[\widetilde{\tau}]\;\;{|}\;\;\varnothing[]\;\;{|}\;\;\#[\widetilde{\tau}]\;\;{|}\;\;\langle l_{i}:\tau_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}\;\;{|}\;\;\texttt{Unit}

At the level of processes, the main difference with respect to the syntax of
\mathsf{HO}\pi
in
Figure

2
is the case construct ‘
\textbf{case}\ v\ \textbf{of}\ \{l_{i}\_(x_{i})\triangleright P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
’, which together with the variant value ‘
l\_v
’ implement a form of deterministic choice. This choice is decoupled from a synchronization: indeed, the process
\textbf{case}\ l_{j}\_v\ \textbf{of}\ \{l_{i}\_(x_{i})\triangleright P_{i}\}_{i\in I}
(with
j\in I
) autonomously reduces to
P_{j}\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$v$}\!/\mbox{\small$x_{j}$}\}
.
At the level of types, the grammar above first defines types

l_{\mathtt{o}}[\widetilde{\tau}]
,
l_{\mathtt{i}}[\widetilde{\tau}]
, and

l_{\#}[\widetilde{\tau}]

for the linear exchange messages of type
\widetilde{\tau}
: output, input, and both output and input, respectively. Then, the types
\varnothing[]
and
\#[\widetilde{\tau}]
are assigned to channels without any capabilities and with arbitrary capabilities, respectively. Finally, we have the variant type
\langle l_{i}:\tau_{i}\rangle_{i\in I}
associated to the case construct and the unit type
Unit
.

As already mentioned, the key idea of the CPS approach is to represent one message exchange using
two
channels: one is the message itself, the other is a continuation for the next sequential action in the session. We illustrate this approach by example.

Example 6.1

(The CPS Approach, By Example)
.

Consider the session type
S
and the processes
Q
and
R
from
Example

5.1
. Under the CPS approach, the session type
S
is represented as linear types as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=l_{\mathtt{i}}[\langle\textsf{add}:{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}S_{\textsf{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket},\ \textsf{neg}:{{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}S_{\textsf{neg}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}}\rangle]

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}S_{\textsf{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=l_{\mathtt{i}}[\textsf{int},l_{\mathtt{i}}[\textsf{int},l_{\mathtt{o}}[\textsf{int},\varnothing[]]]]

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}S_{\textsf{neg}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}

\displaystyle=l_{\mathtt{i}}[\textsf{int},l_{\mathtt{o}}[\textsf{int},\varnothing[]]]

This way, sequentiality in
S
arises in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}S{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}
as nesting of pairs—the later that an action appears in a session type, the deeper it will appear in the corresponding linear type.
Accordingly, the encoding of session-typed processes as linear processes, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{f}
, is illustrated below; the parameter
f
records continuations:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,u)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q\;|\;R{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{\emptyset}=(\nu\,u)\,{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{\emptyset}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle=u?(y).\textbf{case}\ y\ \textbf{of}\ \{\textsf{add}\_c_{1}\penalty\ \triangleright\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q_{\textsf{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{u\mapsto c_{1}},\ \textsf{neg}\_c_{1}\penalty\ \triangleright\penalty\ {\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q_{\textsf{neg}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{u\mapsto c_{1}}\}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q_{\textsf{add}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{u\mapsto c_{1}}

\displaystyle=c_{1}?(a,c_{2}).c_{2}?(b,c_{3}).(\nu\,c_{4})\,c_{3}!\langle a+b,c_{4}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}Q_{\textsf{neg}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{u\mapsto c_{1}}

\displaystyle=c_{1}?(a,c_{2}).(\nu\,c_{3})\,c_{2}!\langle-a,c_{3}\rangle.\mathbf{0}

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}_{\emptyset}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,c_{1})\,u!\langle\textsf{add}\_c_{1}\rangle.(\nu\,c_{2})\,c_{1}!\langle\textbf{16},c_{2}\rangle.(\nu\,c_{3})\,c_{2}!\langle\textbf{26},c_{3}\rangle.c_{3}?(r).\mathbf{0}

Observe how the branching construct in
Q
is encoded using two constructs: an input prefix immediately followed by a case construct.
Also, notice the use of restrictions
(\nu\,c_{i})\,
(with
i\in\{1,2,3\}
) in the encoding of output prefixes: this is crucial to avoid interferences and ensure that session communications are mimicked in the intended order.

Based on the above example, we may identify two sources of comparison between our decompositions into MSTs and the CPS approach.
At the level of types, there is clear resemblance between MSTs and the class of linear types needed to encode finite session types in the CPS approach.
At the level of processes, both approaches use a similar principle, namely to generate fresh names to encode the sequential structure of sessions. While the CPS approach follows a
dynamic
discipline to generate such names (i.e., they are generated by the encoding of output-like actions), the decomposition approach follows a
static discipline
, i.e., fresh names are generated based on the length of the source session types.

\mathsf{HO}\pi
\pi
\mathsf{HO}
\mu\mathsf{HO}\penalty\ {(\mathsf{HO}+\text{MSTs})}
\mu\pi\penalty\ {(\pi+\text{MSTs})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{2}

[
13
,
15
]

.
.

{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\llbracket}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\rrbracket}^{1}_{g}

[
13
,
15
]
{\color[rgb]{0,0.6,0}\mathcal{D}({\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot})}

[
3
,
2
]
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

Figure 18:
Summary of expressiveness results for
\mathsf{HO}\pi
. Solid lines indicate correct encodings and decompositions; dotted lines indicate sub-languages.

7
Concluding Remarks

We studied minimal session types (MSTs) for
\pi
—the sub-language of
\mathsf{HO}\pi

[
13
,
15
]
with first-order communication, recursion, and recursive types—and obtained new minimality results based on two different decompositions of processes.
Introduced in
[
3
]
, MSTs express a specific form of minimality, which eschews from sequentiality in types;
hence, our minimality results for
\pi
mean that sequentiality in types is a convenient and yet not indispensable feature, as it can be represented by name-passing processes while remaining in a session-typed setting.

Following the approach for
\mathsf{HO}

[
3
,
2
]
, which is inspired by Parrow’s trios processes
[
18
]
,
we defined two process decompositions for
\pi
. They use type information to transform processes typable with standard session types into processes typable with MSTs.
The first decomposition, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
, is obtained by composing existing encodability results and the decomposition / minimality result for
\mathsf{HO}
; the second decomposition, denoted
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
, optimizes the first one by (i) removing redundant synchronizations and (ii) using the native support of recursion in
\pi
.
For both decompositions
we establish the minimality result (cf.
Theorems

3.1
and
4.1
), which is actually a result of static correctness (i.e., preservation of typability).
The gains in moving from
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{F}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}
to
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
can be accounted for in very precise terms, as attested by
Lemma

4.1
.
For the optimized decomposition
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathcal{F}^{\ast}(}{\color[rgb]{0,0,0}\cdot}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
we proved also a dynamic correctness result (
Theorem

4.2
), which formally attests that a process and its decomposition are behaviorally equivalent. This result thus encompasses a result of operational correspondence; its proof leverages existing characterizations of contextual equivalence for
\pi

[
14
]
, and adapts them to the case of MSTs.
This way, our technical results together confirm the significance of MSTs; they also indicate that a minimality result is independent from the kind of communicated objects, either abstractions (functions from names to processes, as studied in
[
3
,
2
]
) or names (as studied here).

Sequentiality is the key distinguishing feature in the specification of message-passing programs using session types.
Our minimality results for
\pi
and
\mathsf{HO}
should not be interpreted as meaning that sequentiality in session types is redundant in
modeling and specifying
processes; rather, we claim that it is not an essential notion to
verifying
them.
Because we can type-check session typed
processes using type systems that do not directly support sequentiality in types, our decompositions suggest a technique for implementing session types into languages whose type systems do not support sequentiality.

All in all, besides settling a question left open in
[
3
,
2
]
,
our work deepens our understanding about the essential mechanisms in session-based concurrency and about the connection between the first-order and higher-order paradigms in the typed setting.

Figure

18
depicts the formal connections between
\mathsf{HO}\pi
and its sub-languages, based on: (i) the mutual encodability results between
\pi
and
\mathsf{HO}

[
13
,
15
]
; (ii) the minimality result for
\mathsf{HO}

[
3
,
2
]
; and (iii) the decompositions and minimality results for
\pi
obtained here.

There are several interesting items for future work.
First, it would be interesting to consider asynchronous communication and subtyping in the context of our decompositions, both first-order and higher-order.
Second, following the discussion in
Section

6
,
it would be insightful to formulate a “hybrid” approach that combines and unifies the CPS approach
[
11
,
5
,
6
]
and our approach based on MSTs. Strengths of our approach include direct support for recursion and recursive types, and dynamic correctness guarantees given in terms of well-studied behavioral equivalences; on the other hand, the CPS approach offers a simple alternative to represent non-uniform session structures, such as those present in labeled choices.

Acknowledgments.

We are grateful to the anonymous reviewers and to Dan Frumin for useful comments and suggestions, which helped us to improve our paper.
This research has been supported by the Dutch Research Council (NWO) under project No. 016.Vidi.189.046 (‘Unifying Correctness for Communicating Software’).

Appendix A
Proofs

A.1
Auxiliary Results

We rely on the following properties of the type system in
Section

2.2
.

Lemma A.1

(Substitution Lemma)
.

\Gamma;\Delta,x:S\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
and

u\notin\mathsf{dom}(\Gamma,\Delta)
implies

\Gamma;\Delta,u:S\vdash P\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$u$}\!/\mbox{\small$x$}\}\triangleright\diamond
.

Lemma A.2

(Shared environment weakening)
.

If
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond

then

\Gamma,u:\langle C\rangle;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
.

Lemma A.3

(Shared environment strengthening)
.

If
\Gamma;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
and

u\notin\mathsf{fn}(P)
then
\Gamma\setminus u;\Delta\vdash P\triangleright\diamond
.

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Gamma\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})};{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Delta\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})},{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{H}(}\Delta_{\mu}\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}\vdash(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{r})\,\big(\prod_{r\in\tilde{v}}P^{r}\;|\;\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\rangle.\mathbf{0}\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\mathcal{A}}^{k}_{\epsilon}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big(}P\sigma{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\big)}_{g}\big)

∎

A.3
Proof of
Lemma

4.1

See
4.1

Proof.

Because
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\#(}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0})}\geq{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P)|
(
Definition

4.9
), it suffices to show that

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P)|\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

The proof is by induction on structure of
P
. We show one base case (output prefix followed by
inaction) and
three inductive cases (input, restriction, and parallel composition); other
cases are similar:

•

Case
P=u!\langle x\rangle.\mathbf{0}
. Then

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}=4
,
|\mathsf{brn}(P)|=0
, and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}=2
.
So, we have
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P)|\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.

•

Case
P=u!\langle x\rangle.P^{\prime}
with
P^{\prime}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}
.
By IH we know
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+\mathsf{brn}(P^{\prime})\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}
.
We know
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+3
,
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+1
and
\#_{X}(P)=\#_{X}(P^{\prime})
.
So,

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P^{\prime})|+3\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1>\frac{5}{3}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1)
.

•

Case
P=(\nu\,r:S)\,P^{\prime}
with
\mathsf{tr}(S)
.
Then
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}={\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}
(by
Definition

3.2
)
and
|\mathsf{brn}(P)|=|\mathsf{brn}(P^{\prime})|+1
.
Further, we have
\#_{X}(P)=\#_{X}(P^{\prime})
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}+1
.
Now, by IH
we can
conclude that
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot(|\mathsf{brn}(P)|+1)\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1>\frac{5}{3}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1)
.

•

Case
P=P_{1}\;|\;\ldots\;|\;P_{n}
.
By IH we know

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}

(16)

for
i\in\{1,\ldots,n\}
.
We know

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+(n-1)
,

|\mathsf{brn}(P)|=\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+(n-1)
.
So, we should show

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|+(n-1)\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot\big(\sum_{i=1}^{n}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+(n-1)\big)

(17)

That is,

\sum_{i=1}^{n}({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|+1)\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1\big)

Equivalent to

\sum_{i=1}^{n}({\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|+1)\geq\sum_{i=1}^{n}\big(\frac{5}{3}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1)\big)

We show that for each
i=\{1,\ldots,n\}
the following holds:

{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|+1\geq\frac{5}{3}\cdot({\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1)

That is

A=\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|+1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1}\geq\frac{5}{3}

As
P
is in normal form,
we know that
P_{i}\equiv\alpha.P_{i}^{\prime}

where
\alpha
is some prefix. So,
by
Definition

3.2
and
Definition

4.3
we know that
for some
p^{*}
and
p
we have

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}=p^{*}+{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\lbag}P_{i}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\rbag^{*}}
,
\#_{X}(P_{i})=\#_{X}(P_{i}^{\prime})
,
and
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}=p+{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}^{\prime}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}
.
We can distinguish two sub-cases: (i)
P^{\prime}_{i}\not\equiv\mathbf{0}

and (ii) otherwise.
We consider sub-case (i).
By (
16
) we have:

A\geq\frac{\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+p+1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+p^{*}+1}\geq\frac{5}{3}

We need to find a prefix
\alpha
such that
p
and
\frac{p}{p^{*}}
are the least.
We notice that for
the output prefix we have
p=3
and
\frac{p}{p^{*}}=\frac{3}{1}
.
So, the following holds

\frac{\frac{5}{3}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+4}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+2}=\frac{5}{3}\cdot\frac{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+\frac{12}{5}}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+2}\geq\frac{5}{3}

Now, we consider sub-case (ii) when
P^{\prime}_{i}=\mathbf{0}
.
In this case we have
{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot\mathsf{brn}(P_{i}^{\prime})={\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P^{\prime}_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}=1
.
We pick
p
and
p^{*}
as in the previous sub-case.
So, we have

\frac{{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\lbag}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0.72,0,0}\rbag}+2\cdot|\mathsf{brn}(P_{i})|+1}{{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\#^{*}(}P_{i}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57})}+1}=\frac{1+3+1}{2+1}=\frac{5}{3}

We can then conclude that
inequality (
17
) holds.

∎

A.6
Proof of
Lemma

4.7

For convenience, we define the function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
,
relying on
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
, as follows:

Definition A.1

(Function
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
)
.

Let
P
be a process,
\rho
be a name substitution, and
\sigma
be an indexed
name substitution. We define
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}}^{\rho}_{\sigma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
as follows:

\displaystyle{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}}^{\rho}_{\sigma}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

\displaystyle={\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u_{*}}}_{\tilde{x_{*}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P\sigma{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

\displaystyle\quad\text{ with }\rho=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\},\widetilde{u_{*}}=\mathsf{bn}(\widetilde{u}\sigma:\widetilde{C}),\widetilde{x_{*}}=\mathsf{bn}(\widetilde{x}\sigma:\widetilde{C})

where
\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{C}
.

Recall that
\mathcal{S}
has been defined in
Definition

4.27
.

See
4.7

Proof.

By transition induction.
Let
\rho_{1}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}$}\}
.
By inversion of
P_{1}\,\mathcal{S}\,Q_{1}

we know there is
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{index}(\mathsf{fn}(P_{1})\cup\widetilde{u}\cup\widetilde{x})
,
such that
Q_{1}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}}^{\rho_{1}}_{\sigma_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
.
We need the following assertion on index substitutions.

If
P_{1}\rho_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\ell}}P_{2}\rho_{2}
and
\mathtt{subj}(\ell)=n
then there exists
Q_{2}

such that
Q_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\breve{\ell}}}Q_{2}

with
\mathtt{subj}(\breve{\ell})=n_{i}
and
Q_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\widehat{\mathscr{C}}}^{\rho_{2}}_{\sigma_{2}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

such that
\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{index}(P_{2}\rho_{2})
,

\mathsf{next}(n_{i})\in\sigma_{2}
,
and

\sigma_{1}\cdot(\sigma_{2}\setminus\mathsf{next}(n_{i}))=(\sigma_{2}\setminus\mathsf{next}(n_{i}))\cdot\sigma_{1}
.

We proceed as follows.

•

First, we consider two base cases: Rules
\langle\textsc{Snd}\rangle
and

\langle\textsc{Rv}\rangle
.

•

Then, we consider two inductive cases:
Rules
\langle\textsc{Par${}_{L}$}\rangle
and
\langle\textsc{Tau}\rangle
.
We omit the inductive cases
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{New}\rangle}
and
{\footnotesize\langle\textsc{Res}\rangle}

as they follow directly by the inductive hypothesis and the
definition of the restriction case in
{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

(
Table

7
).

•

Finally, we separately treat cases when a process is recursive.
We show two cases (
\langle\textsc{Rv}\rangle
and
\langle\textsc{Par${}_{L}$}\rangle
)
emphasizing the specifics of the recursion breakdown.

Non-recursive cases

We detail the four cases mentioned above.

1.

Case
\langle\textsc{Snd}\rangle
.

We note that we only consider the case when
P_{1}
is a pure process,
as the case when
P_{1}
is a trigger collection follows directly by
Lemma

4.5
.

We distinguish two sub-cases: (i)

P_{1}=n!\langle v\rangle.P_{2}

and (ii)
P_{1}=n!\langle y\rangle.P_{2}
with

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$v$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}\in\rho_{1}
.
In both sub-cases we know there is

\rho_{2}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}_{2}$}\}
such that

P_{1}\rho_{1}=n\rho_{1}!\langle v\rangle.P_{2}\rho_{2}

We have the following transition:

Snd

P_{1}\rho_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n\rho_{1}!\langle v\rangle}}P_{2}\rho_{2}

Let
\sigma_{1}\in\mathsf{index}(\widetilde{p})
where

\widetilde{p}=\mathsf{index}(\mathsf{fn}(P_{1})\cup\widetilde{u}\cup\widetilde{x})
such that

\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$n_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$n$}\}\in\sigma_{1}
and
\sigma_{2}^{\prime}=\sigma_{2}\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$t_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$t$}\}\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$t_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$t$}\}
.

Here we take
\sigma_{v}=\sigma_{1}
, as we know
v\in\mathsf{fn}(P_{1})
.

Further, let
\widetilde{u_{*}}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{u}\sigma_{1}:\widetilde{C})

and
\widetilde{x_{*}}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}\sigma_{1}:\widetilde{C})
with
\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{C}
.
Also, let
\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fnb}(P_{2},\widetilde{x_{*}}\setminus\widetilde{w})

where
\widetilde{w}=\{n_{i}\}
if
\mathsf{lin}(n_{i})
otherwise
\widetilde{w}=\epsilon
.
Then, in both sub-cases, there are two possibilities for the shape of
Q_{1}
, namely:

\displaystyle Q^{1}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k})\,(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x_{*}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P_{1}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)})

\displaystyle Q^{2}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})\,n_{i}\rho_{*}!\langle\widetilde{v}\rangle.\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rho_{*}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k+1}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P_{2}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

where
v\sigma_{v}\namesrelate\widetilde{v}

and
\rho_{*}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\widetilde{u}_{*}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\widetilde{x}_{*}$}\}

By
Lemma

4.6
we know that
Q^{1}_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}Q^{2}_{1}
.
Thus, we only consider how
Q^{2}_{1}
evolves.
We can infer the following:

Q^{2}_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n_{i}\rho_{*}!\langle\tilde{v}\rangle}}Q_{2}

where
Q_{2}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})\,\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rho_{*}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k+1}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P_{2}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}
. Then, we should show the following:

\displaystyle{(P_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})\rho_{2}}\ \mathcal{S}\ {(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})}

(145)

By
Table

7
we can see that

\displaystyle Q_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{z}\rho_{*}}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{2}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

(146)

Here we remark that our assertion holds by the definition as we have

\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathsf{next}(n_{i})
.

Next, by
Lemma

4.4
we have

(t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})\sigma_{2}\diamond(t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})

That is, we have

\displaystyle(t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{z}\rho_{*}}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}(t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

(147)

Thus, by (
146
) and (
147
) we have

\displaystyle{(Q_{2}\parallel t_{1}\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{m}}v\sigma_{v}{\,:\,}C_{1})}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{z}\rho_{*}}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}(P_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

(148)

Now, by
Definition

4.4
and
Definition

4.7
we know that

\displaystyle\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fnb}(P_{2},\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}:\widetilde{C})\setminus\widetilde{w})=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}_{2}\sigma_{2}:\widetilde{C}_{2})

with
\widetilde{x}_{2}:\widetilde{C}_{2}
.
Similarly, we have

\widetilde{z}\rho_{*}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}_{2}\rho_{2}\sigma_{2}:\widetilde{C}_{2})

Now, by the assumption
\overline{n}\not\in\widetilde{v}

we know
\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\overline{n}_{i}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\overline{n}$}\}\not\in\sigma_{2}
.
Thus, by
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathsf{next}(n_{i})\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$t_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$t$}\}

and
Definition

4.26
, we have

\sigma_{2}\in\mathsf{index}(\mathsf{fn}(P_{2}\parallel t\Leftarrow_{\mathtt{C}}v{\,:\,}C_{1})\cup\widetilde{u}_{2}\cup\widetilde{x}_{2})

By this and (
148
) the goal (
145
) follows.
This concludes
\langle\textsc{Snd}\rangle
case.

2.

Case
\langle\textsc{Rv}\rangle
.

As in above case (
\langle\textsc{Snd}\rangle
), we only consider the case when
P_{1}
is a pure process,
as the case when
P_{1}
is a trigger collection follows directly by

.

Here we know
P_{1}=n?(y).P_{2}
.

\rho_{2}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{u}_{2}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{x}_{2}$}\}

such that

The transition is as follows:

Rv

and
\sigma_{2}=\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathsf{next}(n_{i})\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}
.

\widetilde{u_{*}}=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{u}\sigma_{1}:\widetilde{C})

and

with
\widetilde{u}:\widetilde{C}
.
Also, let

\widetilde{z}=\mathsf{fnb}(P_{2},\widetilde{x_{*}}\widetilde{y}\setminus\widetilde{w})

if
\mathsf{lin}(n_{i})
otherwise
\widetilde{w}=\epsilon
.
Then, there are two possibilities for the shape of
Q_{1}
, namely:

\displaystyle Q^{1}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k})\,(\overline{c_{k}}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k}_{\tilde{x_{*}}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P_{1}\sigma_{1}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)})

\displaystyle Q^{2}_{1}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k+1})\,n_{i}\rho?(\widetilde{y}).\overline{c_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{z}\rho\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\mathscr{A}}^{k+1}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}P_{2}\sigma_{2}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}

4.6
we know that
Q^{1}_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{\tau}}Q^{2}_{1}
.

evolves.
We can infer the following:

\displaystyle Q^{2}_{1}\mathrel{\xrightarrow{n_{i}\rho?\langle\widetilde{v}\rangle}}Q_{2}

and
v\sigma_{v}\namesrelate\widetilde{v}

\sigma_{v}\in\mathsf{index}(v)

\displaystyle P_{2}\rho_{2}\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$v$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}\,\mathcal{S}\,Q_{2}

we can see that

\displaystyle Q_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{z}\rho_{2}\cdot\{\raisebox{1.50694pt}{\small$\tilde{v}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{y}$}\}}_{\tilde{z}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{2}\sigma_{2}\cdot\sigma_{v}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}

We may notice that

\sigma_{2}\cdot\sigma_{v}\in\mathsf{index}(\mathsf{fn}(P_{2})\cup\widetilde{u}_{2}\cup\widetilde{x}_{2})

Futher, by the definition we have

(\sigma_{2}\cdot\sigma_{v})\setminus\mathsf{next}(n_{i})=\sigma_{1}\cdot\sigma_{v}

on index substitutions holds.

By

4.7

\displaystyle\widetilde{z}

\displaystyle=\mathsf{fnb}(P_{2},\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}:\widetilde{C})\cdot\widetilde{y}\setminus\widetilde{w})

\displaystyle=\mathsf{nbd}(\widetilde{x}y(\sigma_{1}\cdot\mathsf{next}(n_{i})\cdot\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$y_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$y$}\}):\widetilde{C}S)

with

. Similarly, we have

(

\sigma_{v}=\sigma_{1}
, otherwise
\sigma_{v}=\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$v_{j}$}\!/\mbox{\small$v$}\}
for
j>0
.
Now, we could see that

Q^{3}_{1}\mathrel{\xRightarrow{n?\langle\tilde{v}\rangle}}Q_{2}

where

\displaystyle Q_{2}=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,V_{1}\;|\;\overline{c^{r}_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime}_{2}\rangle.\mu X.c^{r}_{k}?(\widetilde{y_{*}}_{1}).(\overline{c^{r}_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{y_{*}}_{2}\rangle.X\;|\;c^{r}_{k+l+1}!\langle\widetilde{z_{*}}_{1}\rangle)\;|\;c^{r}_{k+l+1}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime}_{R}\rangle\;|\;V_{2}

We define
Q^{1}_{2}
as follows

\displaystyle Q^{1}_{2}

\displaystyle=(\nu\,\widetilde{c})\,V_{1}\;|\;\overline{c^{r}_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime}_{2}\rangle.\mu X.c^{r}_{k}?(\widetilde{y_{*}}_{1}).(\overline{c^{r}_{k+1}}!\langle\widetilde{y_{*}}_{2}\rangle.X\;|\;c^{r}_{k+l+1}!\langle\widetilde{z_{*}}_{1}\rangle)\;|\;V_{2}\;|\;

\displaystyle\quad(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k})\,(c^{r}_{k}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{z_{*}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{\emptyset})\;|\;(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k})\,(c^{r}_{k}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime}_{R}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{w_{*}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{w}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{z}_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{\emptyset})

By definition, we could see that

Q^{1}_{2}\in{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\mathcal{C}}^{\tilde{u_{*}}_{2}}_{\tilde{z_{*}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big(}P_{2}^{\prime}\;|\;R\;|\;R\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{w}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{z}_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.5,1}\big)}
.
Now,
by the definition of
{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,-}_{-}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}\cdot{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{\emptyset}
we have

\displaystyle c^{r}_{k+l+1}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\rangle\;|\;c^{r}_{k+l+1}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime}_{R}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k+l+1}_{\tilde{z_{*}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{\emptyset}\approx^{\mathtt{C}}

\displaystyle\quad(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k})\,(c^{r}_{k}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime\prime}_{1}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{z_{*}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{\emptyset})\;|\;(\nu\,\widetilde{c}_{k})\,(c^{r}_{k}!\langle\widetilde{u_{*}}^{\prime}_{R}\rangle\;|\;{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k}_{\tilde{w_{*}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R\{\raisebox{2.15277pt}{\small$\tilde{w}_{1}$}\!/\mbox{\small$\tilde{z}_{1}$}\}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{\emptyset}

As each trio in

{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\widehat{\mathscr{A}}}^{\,k+l+1}_{\tilde{z_{*}}_{1}}{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big(}R{\color[rgb]{0,0.44,0.57}\big)}_{\emptyset}
makes a replica of itself
when triggered along a propagator.
So, finally we have

Q_{2}\approx^{\mathtt{C}}Q^{1}_{2}

This concludes the proof of
Lemma

4.7
.

∎
